A Fully Discrete Surface Finite Element Method for the Navier–Stokes equations on Evolving Surfaces with prescribed Normal Velocity
Charles M. Elliott, Achilleas Mavrakis

Abstract




Abstract.
We analyze two fully time-discrete numerical schemes for the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations posed on evolving surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with prescribed normal velocity using the evolving surface finite element method (ESFEM). We employ generalized Taylor-Hood finite elements

\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}
,
k_{u}=k_{pr}+1\geq 2
,
k_{\lambda}\geq 1
, for the spatial discretization, where the normal velocity constraint is enforced weakly via a Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
, and a backward Euler discretization for the time-stepping procedure. Depending on the approximation order of
\lambda
and weak formulation of the Navier-Stokes equations, we present stability and error analysis for two different discrete schemes, whose difference lies in the geometric information needed. We establish optimal velocity
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
-norm error bounds (
a_{h}
an energy norm) for both schemes when
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
, but only for the more information intensive one when
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
, using
iso-parametric
and
super-parametric
discretizations, respectively, with the help of a newly derived surface Ritz-Stokes projection. Similarly, stability and optimal convergence for the pressures is established in an
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{H_{h}^{-1}}
-norm (
H_{h}^{-1}
a discrete dual space) when
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
, using a novel
Leray time-projection
to ensure weakly divergence conformity for our discrete velocity solution at two different time-steps (surfaces). Assuming further regularity conditions for the more information intensive scheme, along with an almost weak divergence conformity result at two different time-steps, we establish optimal
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
-norm pressure error bounds when
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
, using
super-parametric
approximation. Simulations verifying our results are provided, along with a comparison test against a penalty approach.

1.
Introduction

Analysis of fluid equations on
evolving surfaces
has been a growing field in recent years both in an analytic and numerical setting, as they are important in describing many phenomena such as liquid films, bubbles, foams and lipid bilayers
[
5
,
47
,
73
]
. Due to surface evolution, the flow now consists of both in-plane (tangential) and out-of-plane (normal) components, the latter leading to shape changes that are generally unknown
[
75
,
63
,
52
]
. For example, lipid bilayer membranes can be described as a two-dimensional surface whose tangential fluid-like motions can be prescribed by the evolving surface Navier-Stokes equations (eNSE)
[
49
,
53
,
55
]
, while elastic forces or bending energies act in the normal direction
[
75
,
10
,
9
]
, or two phase flows where the interface can be modeled by (eNSE); see
[
40
,
38
,
8
,
1
,
59
]
where the latter is treated numerically. We also refer to
[
7
]
for a general review and additional literature.

A simplified variant can be analyzed by specifying the evolution (normal dynamics) of the surface, which leads to the evolving surface Navier-Stokes equations with prescribed out-of-plane flow (normal velocity) as modeled in
[
49
,
63
]
. Considering this assumption, it only remains to solve for the in-plane fluidic part.
Such a simplified model was studied in
[
66
]
, where well-posedness was addressed for the tangential surface Navier-Stokes equations (TSNSE) posed on a passively evolving surface embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For our numerical purposes, instead of splitting the surface Navier-Stokes equations into in-plane and out-of-plane contributions, we instead treat the equations as vector-valued PDE for the unknown surface velocity
{\mathbf{u}}
, whose normal component is prescribed via a Lagrange multiplier
[
13
,
63
]
:

For an evolving two-dimensional closed, compact and oriented surface
\{\Gamma(t)\}_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
we want to consider a fully discrete evolving surface Taylor-Hood finite element scheme (ESFEM) for the
evolving surface Navier-Stokes equations with extra Lagrange multiplier
:

(eNSEL)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=V_{\Gamma}\\
\rho\partial^{\bullet}{\mathbf{u}}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}p+2\mu\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(E({\mathbf{u}}))+{\bm{f}}-\lambda^{dir}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}=0.\end{cases}

where
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t):\Gamma(t)\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
the velocity field,
p(\cdot,t):\Gamma(t)\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the surface pressure with
\int_{\Gamma(t)}p\,ds=0
, acting as a Lagrange multiplier for the incompressibility condition and
\lambda^{dir}(\cdot,t):\Gamma(t)\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
an extra Lagrange multiplier associated with the constraint of the prescribed normal velocity
{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=V_{\Gamma}
(we explain the superscript
dir
later in
Section

3
). In addition,
\partial^{\bullet}
denotes the material derivative,
\rho
is the density distribution and
\mu
stands for the viscosity coefficient, while
{\bm{f}}\in({L}^{2}(\Gamma(t)))^{3}
is an external vector forcing term. Here the rate-of-strain (deformation) tensor
E(\cdot)

[
63
]
, is defined for an arbitrary vector field
{\mathbf{v}}
, by

(1.1)

\displaystyle E({\mathbf{v}})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}).

where
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}:={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}
(with transpose
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}=(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}})^{t}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
) and
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
denotes the projection to the tangent space of
\Gamma
.

The system (
eNSEL
) was derived in
[
63
]
, as a thin film limit of an annular three dimensional classical Navier-Stokes system. Other derivations for surface Navier-Stokes equations on evolving surfaces, include
[
49
]
, where the authors use conservation laws of surface mass and momentum quantities as physical principles and
[
53
]
where instead such equations were derived by variational
energy principles; we also refer the reader to
[
13
]
for a review regarding such derivations. See also
[
24
]
regarding well-posedness of Oseen equations on moving domains.

In this work, we want to consider a finite element discretization of (
eNSEL
) using
Taylor-Hood
evolving surface finite elements. Surface finite elements methods (SFEM) have been studied extensively in the literature; see
[
27
,
31
,
26
]
for elliptic and parabolic problems on a stationary and evolving surfaces
\Gamma
. There have also been extensions to vector or tensor-valued functions
[
43
,
44
]
. Recently, there have been numerous studies around the surface (Navier)-Stokes equations, including parametric (SFEM)
[
12
,
36
,
72
,
32
,
22
,
23
,
65
,
45
]
and Eulerian (TraceFem-CutFem) approaches, see e.g.
[
16
,
68
,
48
]
. Discretizations of fluid problems on surfaces pose a lot of challenges: Mainly, the simultaneous imposition of an out-of-plane (e.g. tangential) condition (motion) and conformity (continuity) of the discrete velocity vector fields used to approximate solutions in the primitive variables; see
[
58
,
72
,
32
,
45
,
22
,
73
]
and the approximation of geometric quantities, e.g. mean and Gaussian curvature. There has been a plethora of papers and techniques dealing with these issues, each with its own advantages and disadvantages.
For example, approaches that enforce the tangentiality condition strongly have been considered in
[
12
]
, where an
H(\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma})
conforming finite element method with an interior penalty approach to enforce
H^{1}-
continuity weakly is analyzed, while similarly the authors in
[
22
,
23
]
use the surface Piola transforms, instead, to ensure strong tangentiality,
H(\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma})
conformity and also weak
H^{1}
-continuity, without requiring additional consistency terms as before. Furthermore, simpler existing
H^{1}
-conforming
Taylor-Hood
finite element methods have been considered in studies, where instead, the authors enforce the out-of-plane conditions (tangentiality) weakly either by penalization
[
72
,
45
,
68
,
48
]
or by Lagrange multipliers
[
32
,
33
]
. We follow the latter.

As mentioned, we consider simplified models for fluid deformable surfaces, such that the deformation of the surface is prescribed. Recently, there has been considerable interest in the numerical approximations for solutions of such systems
[
55
,
56
,
73
,
69
,
74
]
. However, to the authors’ knowledge there does not seem to be much literature involving numerical (error) analysis of systems like (
eNSEL
) on evolving surfaces, especially using (Lagrangian) parametric FEM. In
[
69
]
, the authors study the discretizations
of the
tangential surface Navier-Stokes equations on a passively involving surfaces
(TSNSE) using an Eulerian formulation of the PDE (unfitted FEM – TraceFem). The TSNSE arises by decoupling the velocity into a tangential and normal component
[
66
,
13
]
. Using TraceFem, the authors were able to study a-priori discretization error bounds and find optimal velocity
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
(
||\cdot||_{a_{h}}
– an energy norm) error estimates. On the other hand, due to the use of Eulerian time-stepping scheme and therefore the lack of weak divergence conformity of velocity solutions at two different time-steps, e.g.
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}\notin{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n,div}
, only a weaker special
L^{2}_{H^{1}}
-norm pressure bound was be obtained, assuming also an inverse (albeit weak) CFL condition. Similar difficulties were observed in the case of Navier-Stokes on moving domains
[
64
,
15
,
76
]
; see also
[
11
]
. Only recently in
[
39
]
has there been a study involving the stability analysis of a numerical method for the Navier-Stokes equation on evolving surface (eNSE) using parametric FEM.

We investigate numerically a similar scheme to
[
55
]
for the full eNSE, where instead of using a penalty-based approach to constraint of the normal component of the velocity, we use a
Lagrange-based approach and therefore consider (
eNSEL
).
Using
H^{1}
-conforming
Taylor-Hood
ESFEM, we prove optimal
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
-norm velocity error estimates. Additionally, compared to the Eulerian FEM approach
[
69
]
, we are also able to prove
L^{2}_{L^{2}}
-norm stability and error bounds for the pressure
p
. This is achieved by using a novel discrete Leray
time-projection

\hat{{\mathbf{u}}}_{h}^{n-1}

Eq.

6.17
on the previous time-step
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}
, so that
\hat{{\mathbf{u}}}_{h}^{n-1}
is weakly divergence free with respect to the next surface but also “close” enough to
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}
. To our knowledge this is the first full error analysis for the Navier–Stokes equations on an evolving surface using parametric finite elements.

1.1.
Main Results

We analyze two fully time-discrete backward Euler numerical schemes of the incompressible surface Navier–Stokes equations on an evolving surface with prescribed normal velocity using the evolving surface finite element method (ESFEM). We employ a generalized Taylor-Hood finite element triple

\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}
,
k_{u}=k_{pr}+1\geq 2
,
k_{\lambda}\geq 1
, to handle the spatial discretization, where the normal velocity constraint is enforced weakly via an extra Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
. By
k_{u}
,
k_{pr}
,
k_{\lambda}
we represent the degree of polynomials used for the velocity field
{\mathbf{u}}
, pressure
p
and extra Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
approximations, respectively. For inf-sup stability reasons we always consider
k_{u}=k_{pr}+1
, see
Lemmas

6.6
and
6.7
. Depending on the richness of
\mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}
, which involves the approximation of
\lambda
, we present a fully discrete stability and error analysis for two different numerical schemes (
eNSWdh
) (for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
) or (
eNSWch
) (both for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
and
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
). The main difference between these two formulations comes down to the choice of convective term and hence the geometric information needed for the approximations; see
Remark

6.2
for more details.

In summary, the main results are the following:

•

Based on discrete weak formulations of (
eNSEL
) (see
Section

3.1
) we expand on some basic results for the surface finite element method
[
28
,
27
,
31
,
54
]
), involving the surface lifting, approximation of geometry and perturbation of newly introduced bilinear forms, including new discrete transport formulae in
Section

4
, necessary to describe the relation between the time-derivative and time-dependent integrals.

•

As with the stationary surface case
[
33
]
, establishing optimal convergence for the
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
-norm of the velocity necessitates the introduction of a
new Ritz-Stokes projection

\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})
over an evolving surface. The main difference now is that the material derivative and the surface Ritz-Stokes projection do not commute, i.e.
\partial^{\circ}_{h}\mathcal{R}_{h}{\mathbf{u}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{h}\partial^{\circ}_{h}{\mathbf{u}}
and therefore it requires further work to establish appropriate approximation properties. Unfortunately, we are only able to show the following suboptimal bounds for the
L^{2}
-norms

\displaystyle||\partial^{\circ}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}-\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}))||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{r_{u}}\sum_{j=0}^{1}||(\partial^{\circ})^{j}{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{k_{u}+1}(\Gamma(t))}\quad\text{ for }k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1,

\displaystyle||\partial^{\circ}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}-\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\ell}({\mathbf{u}}))||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{\widehat{r}_{u}}\sum_{j=0}^{1}||(\partial^{\circ})^{j}{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{k_{u}+1}(\Gamma(t))}\quad\text{ for }k_{\lambda}=k_{u},

with
r_{u}=min\{k_{u},k_{g}-1\}
,
\widehat{r}_{u}=min\{k_{u},k_{g}\}
(see
Lemma

5.8
). However these are still sufficient to establish optimal
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
convergence for the velocity and eventually
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{H_{h}^{-1}}
(or
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
) for the pressure, using
iso-parametric discretization
for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
and
super-parametric discretization
for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
, as is expected in the stationary case
[
32
,
33
,
36
]
. The main issue lies in the fact that even if we consider the tangential solution
{\mathbf{u}}
, the material derivative is no longer tangential, thus a lot of the geometric perturbation results that take advantage of the said tangentiality (
Lemma

4.16
) become void; see
Remarks

5.4
and
5.5
for more details.

•

Using ESFEM we prove
L^{2}_{L^{2}}
-norm stability and optimal convergence for the pressure
p
. However, it depend on the choice of
k_{\lambda}
in the following sense:

– For
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
, considering
standard relatively low regularity conditions
(see
1
, the ones used to prove
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
convergence), we can show stability and optimal error estimates in the discrete
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{H_{h}^{-1}}
-norm for the (
eNSWdh
) scheme (see
Lemmas

6.8
and
7.10
), by introducing a novel discrete Leray
time-projection
(
6.17
), to ensure weakly divergence conformity for our discrete velocity solution at two different time-steps (surfaces), along with the help of a discrete inverse Stokes operator (
6.31
), a newly developed dual energy norm estimate
Lemma

6.5
and the
L^{2}\times H_{h}^{-1}
discrete
inf-sup
condition (
6.41
) (therefore only establishing error bounds in this weaker dual norm for
\lambda_{h}
). Similarly, we can prove results for the second scheme (
eNSWch
), where one uses the
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}

inf-sup
condition
Lemma

6.6
instead to obtain pressure results in the
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
-norm. The difference between the two schemes regards the geometric information needed, i.e. the approximation of the men curvature
\bm{H}_{h}
; see
Remark

6.2
.

– For
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
, on the other hand, we show discrete
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
-norm stability and optimal convergence results (suboptimal w.r.t. the geometric approximations; see
Theorem

7.17
, hence the need of
super-parametric discretization
) only for the second scheme (
eNSWch
) (it is not possible to prove optimal convergence for (
eNSWdh
)), by considering
additional regularity conditions
(see
2
), and finding an estimate for the time-derivative approximations in a stronger norm (see
Section

7.3
), via an “almost” weak divergence conformity result for solutions at two different time-steps (surfaces), along with the
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}

inf-sup
condition
Lemma

6.6
.

1.2.
Outline

The rest of the chapter is organized as follows. Basic notation, e.g. regarding the functions spaces and evolving surfaces is introduced in
Section

2
. The continuous evolving surface Navier-Stokes problems, which we will analyze numerically, are introduced and set-up in
Section

3
, together with new key Transport formulae for moving surfaces, appropriate for our case.
In
Section

4
we present the evolving surface finite element method, adapted to our situation, introduce the
Taylor-Hood
elements and develop the relative notation regarding discrete surfaces, velocities, material derivative etc. We also recall and
expand
some basic results involving surface lifting, approximation of geometry and perturbation of new bilinear forms (including new discrete Transport formulae). A new surface Ritz-Stokes projection is introduced in
Section

5
, along with error bounds involving the material derivative, which are necessary leading up to the a-priori error analysis for our schemes. In
Section

6
we finally state our two fully discrete numerical schemes, which depends on the choice of the approximation space of the extra Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
, i.e.
k_{\lambda}
, and the convective term.
The main stability arguments are also carried out in this section, where we make use of a novel
Leray time-projection
, to ensure weakly divergence conformity for our discrete velocity solution at two different time-steps (surfaces). The main convergence results are presented in
Section

7
, where in the first part we establish optimal
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
velocity error estimates for the respective numerical schemes under
standard regularity assumptions
. Then, we establish optimal pressure error estimates in standard spaces. Specifically, for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
, with the previous
regularity assumptions
in mind, we prove optimal error pressure bounds in an
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{H_{h}^{-1}}
-norm, while for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
, we prove optimal
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
-norm error pressure bounds, only after considering

additional regularity assumptions
. Finally, numerical results are presented in
Section

8
, which support the theoretical results.

2.
Differential geometry on surfaces

We recall some fundamental notions and tools concerning surface vector-PDEs. We mainly follow definitions in
[
43
,
49
,
32
]
.

2.1.
The closed smooth surface

We consider surfaces
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
to be a closed, oriented, compact
C^{m}
two-dimensional hypersurface embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We will require
m\geq 4
in our analysis, see
[
32
, Remark 4.1]
. Note that since
\Gamma
is the boundary of an open set we choose the orientation by setting
{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
to be the unit outward pointing normal to
\Gamma
. Let
d(\cdot)
denote the signed distance functions such that
d\equiv 0
on
\Gamma
. Then, by the smoothness of
\Gamma
, there exists a small enough
\delta
such that
d(\cdot):U_{\delta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is globally Lipchitz-continuous, where

U_{\delta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the tubular neighborhood
U_{\delta}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ :|d(x)|<\delta\}
.
Then we may define the closest point projection map to
\Gamma
as

(2.1)

\pi(x)=x-d(x){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\pi(x))\in\Gamma

for each
x\in U_{\delta}
, and for
\delta>0
small enough, see also
[
41
,
27
]
, where
\nabla d(x)={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\pi(x))
for
x\in U_{\delta}
. We can then see that
d(\cdot)
and
\pi(\cdot)
are of class
C^{m}
and
C^{m-1}
on
\overline{U_{\delta}}
. Using the projection
\pi
we may extend functions
{\mathbf{v}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
on
\Gamma
to the tubular neighbourhood
U_{\delta}
, by

(2.2)

{\mathbf{v}}^{e}(x)={\mathbf{v}}(\pi(x)),\ \ \ x\in U_{\delta}.

This extension is constant in the normal direction of
\Gamma
and thus contains certain useful additional properties compared to other regular extensions. We define the extension of the normal to the neighborhood
U_{\delta}
by
{\mathbf{n}}={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{e}={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\circ\pi
. Furthermore, define the orthogonal projection operator onto the tangent plane
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}(x)={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(x)\otimes{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(x)
for
x\in\Gamma
that satisfies
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}^{T}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}^{2}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
,
|{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}|_{Fr}=2
and
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=0
, with its extension to
U_{\delta}
given by
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}^{e}={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}
. We can readily see that
{\mathbf{n}}|_{\Gamma}={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
and
{\mathbf{P}}|_{\Gamma}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
.

2.2.
Scalar functions and vector fields

We start by defining useful quantities for
scalar
functions following
[
27
]
. For a function
f\in C^{1}(\Gamma)
we define the
tangential (surface) derivative
of
f
as

(2.3)

\nabla_{\Gamma}f(x)={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}(x)\nabla f^{e}(x),\ \ \text{with }\ \underline{D}_{i}f(x)=P_{ij}\partial_{j}f^{e}(x),\ \ \ x\in\Gamma,

where we use the Einstein summation as defined in
Appendix

A
and where
(\nabla f^{e})_{i}=\partial_{i}(f^{e})
,
1\leq i\leq 3
is the Euclidean derivative. Here
f^{e}
is the extension to
U_{\delta}
where
f^{e}|_{\Gamma}=f
as in (
2.2
). Note also that we consider
\nabla_{\Gamma}f(x)=(\underline{D}_{1}f(x),

\underline{D}_{2}f(x),

\underline{D}_{3}f(x))^{t}
to be a column vector.

Now, since
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
is the orthogonal projection, which is symmetric, we have that

\nabla_{\Gamma}f(x)={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}(x)\nabla f^{e}(x)=\nabla^{t}f^{e}(x){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}(x),\ \ \ \ {\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}f=\nabla_{\Gamma}f\ \ \text{on }\Gamma.

As noted in
[
27
, Lemma 2.4]
the definition of the tangential gradient is independent of the extension.

We now introduce surface derivatives for
vector fields
e.g.
{\mathbf{v}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
; for more details see
[
32
]
and
Appendix

A
. Using the Einstein summations described in
Appendix

A
, the
tangential (directional) derivative
is defined via the derivatives in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as

(2.4)

\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}=(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}})_{ij}=(\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})_{ij}=\partial_{l}v^{e}_{i}P_{jl},

which is a
3\times 3
matrix and where we note that
{\mathbf{v}}^{e}={\mathbf{v}}\circ\pi
, the normal extension. With this in mind, we see that the tangential derivative turns out to be the Euclidean derivative corresponding to this extended vector field i.e.
\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}|_{\Gamma}
.

For general vector fields in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we readily see that the surface gradients above are not truly tangential. Consequently, we define the
covariant derivatives
as follows (see also
[
13
,
36
]
)

(2.5)

\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}=({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}})_{ij}=({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}|_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})_{ij}=P_{jk}\partial_{k}v^{e}_{l}P_{li},

which is again a
3\times 3
matrix. Now it is clear that, compared to the tangential derivative defined in (
2.4
), the covariant derivative (
2.5
) is also a tangential tensor field.

We may also define the Weingarten map
\bm{H}
, to quantify the curvature, as

(2.6)

\bm{H}:=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}},\ \text{ with }\ \bm{H}{\mathbf{n}}=0,\ \ \ \bm{H}{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}\bm{H}=\bm{H},

where we observe that
\bm{H}
is a tangential (in-plane) tensor and also
C^{m-2}
and thus bounded on
\Gamma
, see
[
27
,
41
]
. We also denote by
\kappa=tr(\bm{H})
twice the
mean curvature
.

Now, the surface divergence on
\Gamma
is given by

(2.7)

\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{v}})=tr(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}})=tr({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})=P_{ik}\partial_{k}v_{l}^{e}P_{li}=tr(\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})=tr(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}).

One can always split a vector field
{\mathbf{v}}
into a tangent and a normal component
i.e.

{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}_{T}+{\mathbf{v}}_{n}
with
{\mathbf{v}}_{T}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{v}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{n}=({\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})\,{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
, where also the following useful formulae holds

(2.8)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}+\bm{H}v_{n}.

We also define, via (
2.5
), the surface divergence of a tensor function
{\mathbf{F}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
:

(2.9)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}):=\begin{bmatrix}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}_{1,j})\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}_{2,j})\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}_{3,j})\end{bmatrix},\quad j=1,2,3.

By
({\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{v}}
and
({\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{v}}
we denote the following vectors

(2.10)

\displaystyle\big(({\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}){\mathbf{v}}\big)_{i}=v_{j}P_{jk}\partial_{k}v_{i}^{e}=\big(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{v}}\big)_{i}=\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{v}}_{T},

\displaystyle\big(({\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{v}}\big)_{i}=v_{j}P_{jk}\partial_{k}v_{l}^{e}P_{li}=\big(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}\big)_{i}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{v}}_{T},

Finally, for compact surfaces, i.e.
\Gamma
, we present the following integration by parts rule (surface divergence Theorem) (see
[
27
, Theorem 2.10]
) for smooth enough
{\mathbf{v}}
and
\xi
:

(2.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\,d\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}\xi\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma}\kappa\xi({\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})\,d\sigma.

2.3.
Function spaces

We now define function spaces on the surface
\Gamma
. By
L^{p}(\Gamma)
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, we define the function space that consists of functions
\zeta:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that are measurable w.r.t. the surface measure
d\sigma
, endowed with the standard
L^{p}
-norms,
i.e.

||\cdot||_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}
; see
[
27
]
. By
(\cdot,\cdot)_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}
we denote the usual
L^{2}(\Gamma)
inner product with norm
||\cdot||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}=(\cdot,\cdot)^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}
and by
||\cdot||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}
we denote the
L^{\infty}(\Gamma)
norm. Also,
W^{k,p}(\Gamma)
denotes the standard function Sobolev spaces as presented in
[
27
]
, while by
\mathbf{W}^{k,p}(\Gamma)=(W^{k,p}(\Gamma))^{3}
we write the natural extension to vector-valued functions. Specifically, for
p=2
we write
(H^{k}(\Gamma))^{n}
with
n=1,3
, which are in fact Hilbert spaces. So, for a function
\bm{z}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
n=1,3
, the corresponding norm is given by

(2.12)

\displaystyle||\bm{z}||_{H^{k}(\Gamma)}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}||\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}\bm{z}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)},

where
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}
denote all the weak tangential derivative of order
j
, i.e.
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}\bm{z}=\overbrace{\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\cdot\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}}^{\text{ j times}}\bm{z}
. For
k=0
we get the usual
L^{2}-
norm
||\cdot||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}
for scalar or vector-valued functions.

For vector fields
\bm{z}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
the space
\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)=(H^{1}(\Gamma))^{3}
is equipped with the following norm

(2.13)

\displaystyle||\bm{z}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}^{2}=||\bm{z}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+||\nabla_{\Gamma}\bm{z}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}.

In general with
bold
we define vector-valued space, unless specified otherwise. We also set

L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma):=\{\zeta\in L^{2}(\Gamma)|\ \int_{\Gamma}\zeta\,d\sigma=0\},

to be
L^{2}
functions with zero mean value, equipped with the standard
L^{2}
-norm, while the subspace of tangential vector fields is given by

\bm{H}^{1}_{T}:=\{{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\>|\>{\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=0\},

endowed with the
H^{1}
-norm (
2.13
).

2.4.
Evolving surfaces

Let
\Gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a closed, compact
C^{k}
surface,
k\geq 3
. In order to define a collection of closed and compact
evolving hypersurfaces

\{\Gamma(t)\}_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
for each time
t\in[0,T]
we assume the existence of a smooth
C^{k}
trajectory (or flow)
\Phi(\cdot,t):\Gamma_{0}\mapsto\Gamma(t)
of a material points (induced by a velocity field; see (
2.16
) below) such that

\Gamma(t)=\{\Phi({\mathbf{z}},t)\,|\,{\mathbf{z}}\in\Gamma_{0}\}.

We also write
\Phi(\cdot,t)=\Phi_{t}(\cdot)
. This map induces the spacetime manifold

\mathcal{G}_{T}=\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\}.

We also define
{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}({\mathbf{x}},t)
to be the unit normal of
\Gamma(t)
for
({\mathbf{x}},t)\in\mathcal{G}_{T}
and
\pi({\mathbf{x}},t)
the closest point projection of
\Gamma(t)
, see also (
2.14
), defined by

(2.14)

\pi({\mathbf{x}},t)={\mathbf{x}}-d({\mathbf{x}},t){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\pi({\mathbf{x}},t),t)\ \,\in\Gamma(t).

In order to describe the evolution of the surface, we need to specify the velocity of
\Gamma(t)
. In our setting, as we shall see in
Section

3.1
, since the normal component of the velocity
{\mathbf{u}}
is
known
(
eNSEL
), we have
{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=V_{\Gamma}
and therefore our surface evolves with the
normal material velocity (flow)

(2.15)

{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}=V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\in C^{1}([0,T];C^{k}).

We may then, define the normal trajectory mapping
\Phi_{t}(\cdot)
as followed.

Definition 2.1

(Normal trajectory)
.

Let
\Phi_{t}(\cdot):[0,T]\times\Gamma_{0}\mapsto\Gamma(t)
be a purely
C^{k}
geometric diffeomorphism satisfying

(2.16)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\dfrac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}({\mathbf{z}})={\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}\big(\Phi_{t}({\mathbf{z}}),t\big)\qquad\text{ for ${\mathbf{z}}\in\Gamma_{0}$ and $t\in(0,T]$},\\
&\Phi_{0}({\mathbf{z}})={\mathbf{z}},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\ \ \,\text{ for ${\mathbf{z}}\in\Gamma_{0}$},\end{cases}

where
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
the normal material velocity.

This map exists from standard ODE theory. We also define its inverse mapping
\Phi_{-t}(\cdot):=\Phi^{-1}(\cdot,t).
By the assumed smoothness, it is clear that
||{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}||_{C^{k}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C_{V_{\Gamma}}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

2.4.1.
Evolving Banach/Bochner Spaces

We now shall briefly introduce Bochner-type function spaces for evolving hypersurfaces; for more details see
[
3
,
4
]
.

First, let us define the
evolving Banach spaces
, which we denote by
X(t)
. For this purpose, it is necessary to use suitable pushforward (pullback) maps to (and from) standard Banach spaces. This is achieved with the help of the
normal trajectory map
(
2.16
). So, if consider

\eta\in X_{0}=X(0)
, a standard Banach space, and
\chi\in X(t)
for
t\in[0,T]
, e.g.
X(t)=W^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))
, we may define the pushforward map of
\zeta
and pullback of
\chi
by

\displaystyle\phi_{t}\zeta=\zeta\circ\Phi_{-t}\in X(t),\qquad\phi_{-t}\chi=\chi\circ\Phi_{t}\in X_{0}.

This naturally extends to vector-valued functions; see
[
66
]
. So, the pairs
\big(W^{k,p}(\Gamma(t)),\phi_{t}\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
are shown to be compatible in the sense of
[
3
,
4
]
; that is, classical results carry over to the evolving setting.

With these definitions in mind, we can define
evolving Bochner spaces
for
p\geq 1
, as in
[
3
,
4
]
, by

\displaystyle L^{p}_{X}:=\big\{\chi:[0,T]\rightarrow\bigcup X(t)\times\{t\},\,t\mapsto(\overline{\chi}(t),t)\big|\phi_{-t}\overline{\chi}(t)\in L^{p}([0,T];X_{0})\big\},

where we identify
\chi
with
\overline{\chi}
. This space is endowed with the norm

\displaystyle||\chi||_{L^{p}_{X}}^{p}=\int_{0}^{T}||\chi||^{p}_{X(t)}\,dt.

If in fact
X(t)
are Hilbert spaces and
p=2
, the above norm is induced by the
L^{2}
-inner product

\displaystyle(\chi,v)_{L^{2}_{X}}=\int_{0}^{T}(\chi(t),v(t))_{X(t)}\,dt\qquad\forall\ \chi,\,v\in L^{2}_{X}.

Following
[
2
,
3
]
we see that we can make the identification
(L^{2}_{X})^{*}\simeq L^{2}_{X^{*}}
, where by
X^{*}
we denote the dual space. In case where
p=\infty
the space is equippes with the norm

\displaystyle||\chi||_{L^{\infty}_{X}}=\mathrm{ess\,sup}_{t\in[0,T]}||\chi||^{p}_{X(t)}.

Assuming that
\{\Gamma(t)\}_{t\in[0,T]}

evolving hypersurfaces
evolve with normal velocity field
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
, then taking into account the trajectory of the material points (
2.16
), we define an appropriate time-derivative known as
normal material derivative
(This time for a vector-valued function).

Definition 2.2

(Normal material derivative)
.

Let the hypersurface
\{\Gamma(t)\}_{t\in[0,T]}
with flow defined by (
2.16
). For
\bm{\chi}:\mathcal{G}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
a sufficiently smooth vector field, we define the normal material derivative as

(2.17)

\partial^{\circ}\bm{\chi}=\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\bm{\chi}\circ\Phi_{t}\Big)\circ\Phi_{-t}=\phi_{t}\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\phi_{-t}\bm{\chi}\Big).

It is clear that
\partial^{\circ}(\phi_{t}\bm{\chi})=\partial^{\circ}(\bm{\chi}\circ\Phi_{-t})=0
, for
\bm{\chi}\in\Gamma_{0}
. It is also clear that, using the normal extension (
2.2
), the normal material derivative can be expressed as
\partial^{\circ}\bm{\chi}=\bm{\chi}_{t}^{e}({\mathbf{x}},t)+({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}({\mathbf{x}},t)\cdot\nabla)\bm{\chi}^{e}({\mathbf{x}},t)
. This strong definition can be generalized to define a weak material derivative in the sense of
[
3
, Def. 2.28]
.

3.
Variational formulation

3.1.
Problem Set-up and reformulation

In our setting, the normal velocity component
{\mathbf{u}}_{n}=({\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
is prescribed in (
eNSEL
) by
{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=V_{\Gamma}
and so determines the material velocity (out-of-plane flow) of our surface
\Gamma(t)
. In other words, the normal component of
{\mathbf{u}}
also describes the purely geometric (shape) evolution of the surface
\Gamma(t)
, i.e.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}={\mathbf{u}}_{n}=V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
. Of course, this is just a simplification assumption, and a further physical arbitrary tangential velocity, e.g.
{\mathbf{w}}_{T}
, may be present for the in-plane flow of the material points of the surface
\Gamma(t)
; see
[
4
]
. Nevertheless, the tangent part of the velocity in (
eNSEL
) governs the free lateral motion (in-plane motion) of the material surface
[
49
,
63
]
.

Now, notice that the material derivative depends on the full velocity
{\mathbf{u}}
. However, as mentioned, the surface velocity (geometric evolution) of our surface
\Gamma(t)
, i.e.
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
, is completely determined by the normal component of
{\mathbf{u}}
, that is
{\mathbf{u}}_{n}=V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}}={\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
on
\Gamma(t)
; see (
2.16
). So, since the trajectories of the material points of
\Gamma(t)
are completely determined by
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
, we rewrite the material derivative in (
eNSEL
) with the help of the
normal time derivative
(
2.17
) as

(3.1)

\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}=\partial^{\bullet}{\mathbf{u}}-({\mathbf{u}}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}){\mathbf{u}}.

It is clear that only the tangential component of
{\mathbf{u}}
, which describes the free lateral motions, is now
unknown
. So, considering (
eNSEL
) the problem may be formulated as:

Find velocity
{\mathbf{u}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, surface pressure
p:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\int_{\Gamma(t)}pds=0
, and new Lagrange multiplier associated with the constraint of the prescribed normal velocity
\lambda^{dir}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, such that

(eNS)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=V_{\Gamma}\\
\rho\big(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}+({\mathbf{u}}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}){\mathbf{u}}\big)-2\mu\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(E({\mathbf{u}}))+\nabla_{\Gamma}p+\lambda^{dir}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}={\bm{f}}\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}=0,\end{cases}

which now represents a non-linear problem with only
unknown
the tangent part of the velocity
{\mathbf{u}}({\mathbf{x}},t)
and the pressures
p,\,\lambda
, with initial velocity
{\mathbf{u}}(0)={\mathbf{u}}^{0}={\mathbf{u}}_{n}(0)+{\mathbf{u}}_{T}(0)=V_{\Gamma}(0){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}+{\mathbf{u}}_{T}^{0}
. Now
\lambda^{dir}
refers to the fact that the convective term is written with respect to the directional derivative
\nabla_{\Gamma}(\cdot)
.

Furthermore, for numerical analysis purposes, we also need to consider a different formulation for (
eNSEL
). Using (
A.4
) in
Appendix

A
and defining a new pressure as

(3.2)

\lambda^{cov}=\lambda^{dir}+\rho\big(\nabla_{\Gamma}(V_{\Gamma})\cdot{\mathbf{u}}_{T}+(\bm{H}{\mathbf{u}}_{T})\cdot{\mathbf{u}}_{T}\big),

we can rewrite (
eNSEL
) as follows:

Find velocity
{\mathbf{u}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, surface pressure
p:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\int_{\Gamma(t)}pds=0
, and new Lagrange multiplier associated with the constraint of the prescribed normal velocity
\lambda^{cov}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, such that

(eNSc)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=V_{\Gamma}\\
\rho\big(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}+({\mathbf{u}}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{u}}\big)-2\mu\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(E({\mathbf{u}}))+\nabla_{\Gamma}p+\lambda^{cov}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}={\bm{f}}\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}=0,\end{cases}

with initial velocity
{\mathbf{u}}(0)={\mathbf{u}}^{0}={\mathbf{u}}_{n}(0)+{\mathbf{u}}_{T}(0)=V_{\Gamma}(0){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}+{\mathbf{u}}_{T}^{0}
. Once again, only the tangential component of
{\mathbf{u}}
is
unknown
. We denote the new Lagrange multiplier (pressure) by
\lambda^{cov}
since the convective term is written with respect to the covariant derivative
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}(\cdot)
. We also note that this system (
eNSc
) reduces to the system studied in
[
33
]
when
V_{\Gamma}=0
, i.e. when the surface is stationary.

From now on, for convenience, we set the
density distribution

\rho=1
and the
viscosity parameter

\mu=1/2
. Furthermore, from now on, we will simply denote both
\lambda^{dir}
of (
eNS
) and
\lambda^{cov}
of (
eNSc
) as
\lambda
, since in both cases it acts as a Lagrange multiplier for the normal constraint. Lastly, we see that the incompressibility condition implies the following inextensibility condition

(3.3)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\Gamma(t)|=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}=0,

and knowing that
\int_{\Gamma(t)}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{u}})=0
from the integration by parts formula (
2.11
), we also have

(3.4)

\int_{\Gamma(t)}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}_{n}=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\kappa V_{\Gamma}=0.

3.2.
Bilinear forms

We now introduce an abstract notation for the bilinear forms that will be used throughout the rest of the text.

Definition 3.1
.

For
{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{z}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))
,
q,\xi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
we define the following bilinear forms

\displaystyle{\mathbf{m}}(t;{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}{\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,\qquad\qquad\ {\mathbf{g}}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}):=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}){\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,

\displaystyle a_{S}(t;{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}E({\mathbf{w}}):E({\mathbf{v}})\,d\sigma,\qquad\quad\ a(t;{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}):=\int_{\Gamma(t)}E({\mathbf{w}}):E({\mathbf{v}})\,d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma(t)}{\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,

\displaystyle c^{cov}(t;{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}(({\mathbf{z}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{w}}){\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,\qquad\ \!\!\!c(t;{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}):=\int_{\Gamma(t)}(({\mathbf{z}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}){\mathbf{w}}){\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,

\displaystyle d(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\,d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\,d\sigma.

\displaystyle b^{L}(t;{\mathbf{w}},\{q,\xi\})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}q\,d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\xi{\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\,d\sigma,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},\{q,\xi\})

\displaystyle:=m(\xi,{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})+g({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};\xi,{\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})+\int_{\Gamma(t)}{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{div}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\nabla_{\Gamma}q\,d\sigma,

where
\mathcal{B}_{(\cdot)}^{(\cdot)}(\bullet,\bullet)
the deformation tensors are defined in
Lemmas

B.1
and
B.2
. Furthermore, we define
{\mathbf{d}}(t;\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
as in (
B.17
).

We will usually omit the arguments with (t) for clarity reasons, unless we state otherwise. Now, the analysis over an evolving surface necessitates a form of transport theorem. With the help of
Appendix

B
we have the following.

Lemma 3.1

(Transport Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(t)
be an evolving surface with velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
. For function
f:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
sufficiently smooth we we have

(3.5)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma(t)}f\,d\sigma=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\partial^{\circ}f+f\,\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\,d\sigma.

Furthemore, for
{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))
and
q,\xi,\partial^{\circ}q,\partial^{\circ}\xi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
, the following transport formulae hold:

(3.6)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbf{m}}({\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{m}}(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})+{\mathbf{m}}({\mathbf{w}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}})+{\mathbf{g}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

(3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}b^{L}({\mathbf{w}},\{q,\xi\})

\displaystyle=b^{L}(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}},\{q,\xi\})+b^{L}({\mathbf{w}},\partial^{\circ}\{q,\xi\})+\tilde{b}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},\{q,\xi\}),

(3.8)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{d}{dt}(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}},\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}})_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}},\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}})_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}},\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}})_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+d({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}a_{S}({\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=a_{S}(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})+a_{S}({\mathbf{w}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}})+{\mathbf{d}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}a({\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=a(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})+a({\mathbf{w}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}})+{\mathbf{g}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})+{\mathbf{d}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}).

We note that an explicit expression for
{\mathbf{d}}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
is also given in
Eq.

B.17
.

3.3.
Variational formulation

We wish to derive a weak formulation for both (
eNS
) and (
eNSc
). For reasons of brevity, we use the shorthand notation
c^{(\cdot)}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
to denote either
c(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
or
c^{cov}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
and, as we mentioned, we use a common notation for the extra Lagrange multiplier associated with the normal constraint
\lambda
. This way, we can define one concise weak formulation.

So, testing (
eNS
) and (
eNSc
) with appropriately smooth test functions
{\mathbf{v}}
and
\{q,\xi\}
, using a common notation for
\lambda
, the integration by parts (
2.11
) (see also
[
36
, Eq. (2.3)]
), and setting

(3.11)

\displaystyle\bm{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=(-\kappa V_{\Gamma},V_{\Gamma}),

we define the
weak formulation of the evolving surface Navier-Stokes with extra Lagrange multiplier
:

(eNSW)
: Given
\{{\bm{f}},\bm{\eta}\}\in L^{2}_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}\times(L^{2}_{L^{2}_{0}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}})
we seek solution
{\mathbf{u}}\in L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}\cap L^{2}_{\bm{H}^{1}}\cap H^{1}_{\bm{H}^{-1}}
, and
\{p,\lambda\}\in L^{2}_{L^{2}_{0}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
satisfying the initial condition
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0)={\mathbf{u}}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Gamma(0))
such that,

(eNSW)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\mathbf{m}}(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})+a_{S}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +c^{(\cdot)}({\mathbf{u}};{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +\!\!\!\!&b^{L}({\mathbf{v}},\{p,\lambda\})={\mathbf{m}}({\bm{f}},{\mathbf{v}}),\\
&b^{L}({\mathbf{u}},\{q,\xi\})=m(\bm{\eta},\{q,\xi\}),\end{cases}

for all
{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\,\{q,\xi\}\in(L_{0}^{2}(\Gamma(t))\times L^{2}(\Gamma(t)))
and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where
m(\bm{\eta},\{q,\xi\})=m(\eta_{1},q)+m(\eta_{2},\xi).
For well-posedness of (
eNSW
) we can follow similar computations as in the tangential evolving surface Navier-Stokes in
[
66
]
. Other ways of proving well-posedness, may involve a Rothe-type method, by discretizing the time-derivative first, appropriately. Moreover, in our case, we instead have to use a new inf-sup that also involves the extra Lagrange
\lambda
; see for example
[
32
, Lemma 3.3]
(similarly we can prove an evolving analogue, as in
[
66
, Lemma 3.3]
). Nonetheless, we omit further details, and assume that the following a-priori estimates hold,

(3.12)

\displaystyle||\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{2}_{\bm{H}^{-1}}}+||{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{2}_{\bm{H}^{1}}}+||\{p,\lambda\}||_{L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}}\leq||{\mathbf{u}}_{0}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+||{\bm{f}}||_{L^{2}_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}}+||\bm{\eta}||_{L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}}.

Remark 3.2

(About the source term
\bm{\eta}
)
.

Given the solution
\{{\mathbf{u}},p,\lambda\}
with data
\{{\bm{f}},\bm{\eta}\}
the problems
may be rewritten using the substitution
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}={\mathbf{u}}-\eta_{2}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}-\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi
where
\Delta_{\Gamma}\phi=\eta_{1}=\kappa V_{\Gamma}
. With this substitution we obtain the same equations for
\{\tilde{\mathbf{u}},\,\tilde{p},\,\tilde{\lambda}\}
with new data
\{\tilde{\bm{f}},\bm{\tilde{\eta}}=0\}
and an extra zero order term
V_{\Gamma}\bm{H}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}
. Since the new term can be handled easily in the numerical analysis (see e.g.
[
66
, Section 4.3]
), for reasons of brevity and clarity, we will not include it and simply analyse the numerical discretizations for the systems setting
\bm{\eta}=0
. In
Remark

6.1
this is discussed further.

Thus, for our analysis of numerical discretization in
Section

6.2
and
Section

7
, we study the following two
solenoidal
weak formulations.

The
directional weak formulations of the evolving surface Navier-Stokes with extra Lagrange multiplier
is the following:

(eNSW
d
):
Determine
{\mathbf{u}}\in L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}\cap L^{2}_{\bm{H}^{1}}\cap H^{1}_{\bm{H}^{-1}}
, and
\{p,\lambda\}\in L^{2}_{L^{2}_{0}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
where
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0)={\mathbf{u}}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Gamma(0))
such that,

(eNSW
d
)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\mathbf{m}}(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})+a_{S}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +c({\mathbf{u}};{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +\!\!\!\!&b^{L}({\mathbf{v}},\{p,\lambda\})={\mathbf{m}}({\bm{f}},{\mathbf{v}}),\\
&b^{L}({\mathbf{u}},\{q,\xi\})=0,\end{cases}

for all
{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\,\{q,\xi\}\in(L_{0}^{2}(\Gamma(t))\times L^{2}(\Gamma(t)))
and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

The
covariant weak formulations of the evolving surface Navier-Stokes with extra Lagrange multiplier
is the following:

(eNSW
c
):
Determine
{\mathbf{u}}\in L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}\cap L^{2}_{\bm{H}^{1}}\cap H^{1}_{\bm{H}^{-1}}
, and
\{p,\lambda\}\in L^{2}_{L^{2}_{0}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
where
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0)={\mathbf{u}}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Gamma(0))
such that,

(eNSWc)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\mathbf{m}}(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})+a_{S}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +c^{cov}({\mathbf{u}};{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +\!\!\!\!&b^{L}({\mathbf{v}},\{p,\lambda\})={\mathbf{m}}({\bm{f}},{\mathbf{v}}),\\
&b^{L}({\mathbf{u}},\{q,\xi\})=0,\end{cases}

for all
{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\,\{q,\xi\}\in(L_{0}^{2}(\Gamma(t))\times L^{2}(\Gamma(t)))
and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

Notice that the name
directional
and
covariant
pertains to the different convective term
c(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
and
c^{cov}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
respectively of the two weak formulations.

Remark 3.3
.

We can rewrite
c(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
,
c^{cov}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
in
Definition

3.1
in a skew-symmetric type form. Indeed, starting with (
eNSW
), using integration by parts (
2.11
), the fact that
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{u}})=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{u}}_{T})+({\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})\kappa
and the definition of
{\bm{\eta}}
(
3.11
), we derive the following for
{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))

(3.13)

\displaystyle c({\mathbf{u}};{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Gamma(t)}(({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}){\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma-\int_{\Gamma(t)}(({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}){\mathbf{v}}){\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big)-\frac{1}{2}m(\eta_{1}{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}).

In a similar manner, and also relying on the fact that
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{u}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{u}}_{T}+({\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})\bm{H}
, we derive that

\displaystyle c^{cov}({\mathbf{u}};{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Gamma(t)}(({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{u}}_{T}){\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma(t)}(({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{v}}_{T}){\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big)

(3.14)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\Gamma}\eta_{2}{\mathbf{u}}_{T}\cdot\bm{H}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}-\frac{1}{2}m(\eta_{1}{\mathbf{u}}_{T},{\mathbf{v}}_{T}).

Since, as mentioned in
Remark

3.2
, we will analyze (
eNSWd
) or (
eNSWc
), we consider
\bm{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=0
. That means that the expression
outside the large brackets
in (
3.13
) and (
3.3
) will be zero, and therefore the trilinear forms are now skew-symmetric. Its these consistent modifications that we discretize later in
Definition

4.1
, and
Section

6.2
or eventually in the numerical results; see
Section

8
.

4.
Setup: evolving surface finite element method

4.1.
Recap: Evolving Triangulated Surfaces

We want to approximate our smooth evolving surface
\Gamma(t)
by an appropriate high order discretization. To begin with, we approximate our initial surface
\Gamma(0)
by a polyhedral approximation (usually with the help of a
k_{g}
-order Lagrange interpolation), denoted
\Gamma_{h}(0)
,
contained in the tubular neighborhood
U_{\delta}
i.e.
\Gamma_{h}(0)\subset U_{\delta}
, whose nodal points
\{\alpha_{j}(0)\}_{j=1}^{J}
lie on the surface
\Gamma(0)
.
These nodes define an admissible and conforming (see
[
31
, Section 6.2]
) triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}(0)
such that

\Gamma_{h}(0)=\bigcup_{T(0)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(0)}T(0).

See
[
32
,
26
,
27
,
20
]
for specific construction. Then these nodes of the initial triangulation move along
\Gamma(t)
with our prescribed velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
, i.e. solve the ODE

d\alpha_{j}(t)/dt={\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}(\alpha_{j}(t),t)\qquad j=1,...,J\quad\text{ for }t\in[0,T],

where
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
is the velocity field associated with the evolution of
\Gamma(t)
; see (
2.16
). Therefore, the nodes lie on
\Gamma(t)
for all
t\in[0,T]
and thus also define (by construction) an admissible and conforming triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
such that

\Gamma_{h}(t)=\bigcup_{T(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)}T(t).

See
[
31
, Section 9.5]
for an exact construction of the evolving triangulation. We denote the discrete spacetime surface as

\mathcal{G}_{h,T}=\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\Gamma_{h}(t)\times\{t\}.

We denote by
h_{T(t)}
the diameter of a simplex
T(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
and set
h:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\text{max}\{h_{T(t)}\,:\,T(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)\}
. Considering the assumptions of the smoothness of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
, we may assume that the evolving triangulated surface is
uniformly quasi-uniform
(see
[
31
, Section 6.2, Prop. 9.8]
), that is, the velocity is such that the simplicies in
\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
do not become too distorted, or more formally,
\exists\,\rho>0
such that for all
t\in[0,T]
and
h\in(0,h_{0})
for sufficiently small
h_{0}
we have

(4.1)

min\big\{\rho_{T(t)}\ |\ T(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)\big\}\geq\rho h,

where
\rho_{T(t)}
the standard 2-dim ball contained in each triangle
T(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
, as long as, the initial mesh
\Gamma_{h}(0)
is also. This is important for the extension of the inf-sup to evolving triangulated surfaces
\Gamma_{h}(t)
.

We now denote element-wise outward unit normal to
\Gamma_{h}
by
{\mathbf{n}}_{h}
and define the discrete projection
{\mathbf{P}}_{h}
onto the tangent space of
\Gamma_{h}
by

{\mathbf{P}_{h}}(x)={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h}(x)\otimes{\mathbf{n}}_{h}(x),\quad x\in T,\text{ where }T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}.

As such, one can define the discrete tangential surface operators
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}},\,\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}
analogous to the continuous case; see (
4.37
). Also, the corresponding discrete Weingarten map is
\bm{H}_{h}:=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}.

For the curved triangulation, we may also consider the set of all the edges of the triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
, denoted by
\mathcal{E}_{h}(t)
.
For each edge, we define the outward pointing unit co-normals
\bm{\mu}_{h}^{\pm}
with respect to the two adjacent triangles
T^{\pm}
. Notice that on discrete surfaces in general

[\bm{\mu}_{h}]|_{E}=\bm{\mu}_{h}^{+}+\bm{\mu}_{h}^{-}\neq 0.

For more details on the construction of the triangulated surface see
[
32
,
31
]
and references therein.

4.2.
Finite Element Spaces and Discrete Material Derivative

Certain quantities on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
, e.g. projections and their derivatives, are only defined elementwise on each surface finite element
T
, therefore, it is convenient to introduce broken surface Sobolev spaces on
\Gamma_{h}
, with their respected norm
[
31
]
. As a discrete analogue to
Section

2.3
, the norm
||\cdot||_{H^{m}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}
related to the
broken Sobolev space

(H^{m}(\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)))^{n}
, with
n=1,3
and is defined by

(4.2)

\displaystyle||\bm{z}||_{H^{m}(\mathcal{T}_{h}(t))}^{2}=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)}||\bm{z}||^{2}_{H^{m}(T)}

for a vector field
\bm{z}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h}(t))^{n}
. When a quantity belongs to
\Gamma_{h}(t)
then we replace
\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
with
\Gamma_{h}(t)
.

For every
t\in[0,T]
and
\Gamma_{h}(t)
we now define the space of
H^{1}-
conforming Lagrange finite elements, which also evolves with time, as

(4.3)

S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t):=\{v_{h}\in C^{0}(\Gamma_{h}(t)):v_{h}|_{T(t)}=\hat{v}_{h}\circ\hat{F}_{T(t)}^{-1}\text{ for some }\hat{v}_{h}\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\hat{T}),\ \text{ for all }T(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)\},

where
\mathbb{P}^{k}(\hat{T})
is the space of piecewise polynomials on the reference element
\hat{T}
of degree
k
and
\hat{F}_{T(t)}
is some reference map to some reference element
\hat{T}
also depending on time; see
[
32
, Section 4]
,
[
31
, Definition 6.25, Section 9.2]
. Note also that
S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t)\subset C^{0}(\Gamma_{h}(t))\cap H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
.

This finite element subspace is spanned by continuous evolving basis functions (nodal basis) of order
k
denoted by
\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k}(\cdot,t)
,
j=1,...,J
, that satisfy
\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k}(\alpha_{i}(t),t)=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,\,j=1,...,J
, so that
S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t)=\mathrm{span}\{\chi_{1,k_{g}}^{k}(\cdot,t),...,\chi_{J,k_{g}}^{k}(\cdot,t)\},
where
J
the number of vertices. All the above are easily extended to
vector-valued functions
where now the finite element space takes the form:

(4.4)

(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t))^{3}=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t)\times S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t)\times S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t)\subset\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t)).

For later use, we denote the velocity approximation order by
k_{u}
, and for the pressures
p
and
\lambda
, we represent their approximation orders as
k_{pr}
and
k_{\lambda}
, respectively. So, we denote the appropriate velocity and pressure spaces as

{\mathbf{V}}_{h}(t):=(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{u}}(t))^{3},\ Q_{h}(t):=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{pr}}(t),\ \Lambda_{h}(t):=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{\lambda}}(t),

where, we now write
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}
to denote the extended
Taylor-Hood
surface finite elements. Let us also define the space of
discrete weakly tangential divergence-free functions

(4.5)

{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}(t):=\{\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}(t):b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{w}_{h},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})=0,~~\forall\,\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\}\in Q_{h}(t)\times\Lambda_{h}(t)\}.

We also need the dual space of the finite element space
\Lambda_{h}(t)
, which we denote as

H_{h}^{-1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
. For
\ell_{h}\in\Lambda_{h}(t)
we define its dual norm as

(4.6)

||\ell_{h}||_{H_{h}^{-1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}=\sup_{\xi_{h}\in\Lambda_{h}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{<\ell_{h},\xi_{h}>_{H^{-1},H^{1}}}{||\xi_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}}=\sup_{\xi_{h}\in\Lambda_{h}(t)\backslash\{0\}}\frac{(\ell_{h},\xi_{h})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}}{||\xi_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}}.

Later in
Section

6
we will address the particular choice of parameter choices.

Using the above definitions allows us to characterize the velocity of an arbitrary material point
{\mathbf{x}}_{h}(t)
on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, i.e. the
discrete material (fluid) velocity

{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
that governs the evolution of
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, as the Lagrange interpolant of fluid velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
on the nodes
\{\alpha_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{J}
, given by

(4.7)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\mathbf{x}}_{h}(t)={\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}({\mathbf{x}}_{h}(t),t):=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\dot{\alpha}_{j}(t)\,\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k_{u}}({\mathbf{x}}_{h}(t),t)=\sum_{i=1}^{J}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}(\alpha_{j}(t),t)\,\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k_{u}}({\mathbf{x}}_{h}(t),t),

for
{\mathbf{x}}_{h}\in\Gamma_{h}(t)
, where
\{\{\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k_{u}}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{3}\}_{j=1}^{J}
the nodal basis of
(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{u}}(t))^{3}={\mathbf{V}}_{h}(t)
(
\alpha_{j}(t)
is a vector-valued quantity) and where we write
\dot{\alpha}_{j}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\alpha_{j}(t)
. Notice that despite
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
being purely normal,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
as an interpolant is not, nonetheless we still choose to depict the discrete material velocity with a superscript
N
.
Since
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
is the Lagrange interpolant of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
, it is clear that (see also
[
31
, Lemma 9.18]
),

(4.8)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq c||{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}||_{W^{1,\infty}(\Gamma(t))}.

This evolution, in turn, as in the continuous case
Section

2.4
, induces a discrete analogue flow map
\Phi_{t}^{h}:[0,T]\times\Gamma_{h}(0)\mapsto\Gamma_{h}(t)
from which we write the pushforward and pullback of
\chi_{h}\in\Gamma_{h}(0)
and
\zeta_{h}\in\Gamma_{h}(t)
as

(4.9)

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{h}\chi_{h}=\chi_{h}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{h}\in\Gamma_{h}(t)\qquad\phi_{-t}^{h}\zeta_{h}=\zeta_{h}\circ\Phi_{t}^{h}\in\Gamma_{h}(0),

and so one defines the
discrete strong normal time derivative
by

(4.10)

\partial^{\circ}_{h}\zeta_{h}=\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\zeta_{h}\circ\Phi_{t}^{h}\Big)\circ\Phi_{-t}^{h}=\phi_{t}^{h}\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\phi_{-t}^{h}\zeta_{h}\Big),

for a sufficiently smooth discrete function
\zeta_{h}
.
It is also convenient to introduce the space

\displaystyle S_{h,k_{g}}^{k,C}:=\{z_{h},\,\partial^{\circ}_{h}z_{h}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{G}_{h,T})\big|z_{h}(\cdot,t)\in S_{h,k_{g}}^{k},\,t\in[0,T]\}.

A key result of the evolving surface finite element method is that the discrete material derivative of the nodal basis functions on
\Gamma_{h}(t)
satisfy the
transport property
, shown in
[
25
,
28
]
:

(4.11)

\partial^{\circ}_{h}\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k}=0,\quad\text{for }j=1,...,J.

This result is crucial during the implementation of our numerical scheme. It also implies that for
z_{h}\in S^{k}_{h,k_{g}}
, such that
z_{h}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}z_{j}(t)\chi^{k}_{j,k_{g}}(\cdot,t)
, where
\{z_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{J}
nodal values, the
discrete normal time derivative
can, instead, be written as

(4.12)

\displaystyle\partial^{\circ}_{h}z_{h}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\dot{z}_{j}(t)\chi^{k}_{j,k_{g}}(\cdot,t),

where we write
\dot{z}_{j}=\frac{d}{dt}z_{j}(t)
. Therefore, we see that
\partial^{\circ}_{h}z_{h}\in S^{k}_{h,k_{g}}
and if the nodal values
\{z_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{J}
are sufficiently smooth in times, then
\partial^{\circ}_{h}z_{h}\in S_{h,k_{g}}^{k,C}
. All of the above naturally extend to vector-valued functions e.g. for
\bm{z}_{h}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\dot{\bm{z}}_{j}(t)\chi^{k}_{j,k_{g}}(\cdot,t)\in(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t))^{3}
with vector nodal values
\{\bm{z}_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{J}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

4.3.
Lifts to Exact Surface

Now to relate quantities between the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}(t)
and the continuous one
\Gamma(t)
we want to make use of a lift operator defined using the time-dependent closest point operator
\pi(\cdot,t)
(
2.14
). For that we assume, from now on, that
h\in(0,h_{0})
with
h_{0}
sufficiently small, such that
\Gamma_{h}(t)\in U_{\delta}(\Gamma(t))
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

So, for any (scalar :
n=1
or vector-valued :
n=3
) finite element function
\bm{z}_{h}:\mathcal{G}_{h,T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define its
lift

\bm{z}_{h}^{\ell}:\mathcal{G}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
onto
\Gamma(t)
by

(4.13)

\bm{z}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x,t),t):=\bm{z}_{h}(x,t),\quad\text{for }x\in\Gamma_{h}(t).

Similarly to the extension (
2.2
), we also define the
inverse lift
operator for a function
\bm{z}:\mathcal{G}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

(4.14)

\bm{z}^{-\ell}(x,t):=\bm{z}(\pi(x,t),t),\quad\text{for }x\in\Gamma(t).

Notice that the two extensions (
2.2
) and (
4.14
) agree on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, i.e.
\bm{z}^{-\ell}=\bm{z}^{e}|_{\Gamma_{h}}
since we have that
\Gamma_{h}(t)\subset U_{\delta}(\Gamma(t))
.

Again from the assumption that
\Gamma_{h}(t)\subset U_{\delta}(\Gamma(t))
for all
t\in[0,T]
, the projection
\pi(\cdot)
is well-defined and a bijection. Therefore, for every triangle in the triangulation
T(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
there exists an induced curvilinear triangle
T^{\ell}(t)=\pi(T(t),t)\subset\Gamma(t)
. We further assume that the evolving triangulation is exact, that is,
\mathcal{T}_{h}^{\ell}(t)
forms a conforming subdivision of
\Gamma
; see
[
31
, Definition 7.7]
:

\Gamma(t)=\bigcup_{T^{\ell}(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{\ell}(t)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!T^{\ell}(t).

Then, we may introduce the lifted finite element spaces

(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t))^{\ell}=\{z_{h}^{\ell}\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t)):z_{h}\in S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t)\},

where we note that
\{(\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k})^{\ell}(\cdot,t)\},

j=1,...,J
forms the lifted nodal basis of
(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t))^{\ell}.
The vector-valued quantities are defined in a similar manner.

Geometric Approximations.
The discretization of the continuous surface
\Gamma(t)
, by a discrete triangulated surface
\Gamma_{h}(t)
described in
Section

4.1
, introduces geometric errors. We now present some key geometric error estimates found in
[
54
,
28
,
27
,
26
,
61
]
. In our case, for the numerical study of the evolving surface Navier-Stokes equations, we also need to derive some new geometric estimates involving the material derivative of the discrete normal
{\mathbf{n}}_{h}
and projection
{\mathbf{P}_{h}}
.
Define
{\mathbf{P}},{\mathbf{n}}
the extension of
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}},\,{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
to the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
.

Lemma 4.1

(Geometric Errors)
.

Let
\Gamma(t)
and
\Gamma_{h}(t)
be as described above. Then for sufficiently small
h
we have

(4.15)

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{(k)}d||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},\quad

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{(k)}({\mathbf{n}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||\bm{H}-\bm{H}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}-1},

(4.16)

\displaystyle||[\bm{\mu}_{h}]||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||{\mathbf{P}}[\bm{\mu}_{h}]||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{2k_{g}}.

As a consequence, we also have the following

(4.17)

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{(k)}({\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}_{h}})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||\partial_{h}^{(k)}({\mathbf{P}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||1-{\mathbf{n}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}

(4.18)

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{(k)}({\mathbf{P}_{h}}{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad||\partial_{h}^{(k)}({\mathbf{P}}{\mathbf{P}_{h}}{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},

where the superscript
(k)
denotes the
k
-th discrete normal material derivative, and the constant
c>0
is independent of
h,\,t
.

Proof.

A proof for (
4.16
) is given in
[
67
]
. The rest of the results for
k=0
appear in numerous literature
[
27
,
20
]
and references therein. For
k\geq 1
, most of the results can be found in
[
54
,
61
,
28
]
etc. Specifically, let us first focus on the second estimate in (
4.15
) for
k=1
, which has not been proved, but it is an immediate sequence of already known results. Then, for
k>1
the result follows with similar arguments.

As in
[
61
,
28
]
we consider
T(t)\in\Gamma_{h}(t)
a single element and w.l.o.g. we assume that
T\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
. There, it was proven that

(4.19)

\displaystyle||\partial^{\circ}_{h}n_{j}||_{L^{\infty}(T)}\leq ch^{k_{g}}\text{ for }j=1,2,

and
\partial^{\circ}_{h}n_{3}={\mathcal{O}}(h^{k_{g}+1})
, where
{\mathbf{n}}=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}),
. Then, since by construction
{\mathbf{n}}_{h}={\mathbf{e}}_{3}=(0,0,1)
, we clear see that

(4.20)

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{\circ}({\mathbf{n}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}\leq||\partial_{h}^{\circ}{\mathbf{n}}||\leq ch^{k_{g}}.

Now using the fact that
{\mathbf{P}}={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}
and
{\mathbf{P}}_{h}={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}_{h}
it clear that

(4.21)

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}_{h}})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}\leq ch^{k_{g}}.

The estimate
||\partial_{h}^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}}
was proven similarly in
[
28
, Lemma 5.4]
. From this, we see that

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}_{h}}{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}=||\partial_{h}^{\circ}(({\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}_{h}){\mathbf{P}})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}

(4.22)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\ \leq c||{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes\partial_{h}^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}+||\partial_{h}^{\circ}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes({\mathbf{P}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}\leq ch^{k_{g}},

(4.23)

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}}{\mathbf{P}_{h}}{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}\leq||\partial_{h}^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{P}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})||_{L^{\infty}(T)}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},

which completes the proof.
∎

Let
\mu_{h}(x,t)
be the Jacobian of the transformation
\pi(x,t)|_{\Gamma_{h}}
:
\Gamma_{h}(t)\rightarrow\Gamma(t)
such that
\mu_{h}(x,t)d\sigma_{h}=d\sigma
, then the following estimate also holds

(4.24)

||1-\mu_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},

see for instance
[
28
,
20
]
.

Now, let us compare functions and surface differential operators defined on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
and the exact surface
\Gamma
. Set
{\bm{B}_{h}}={\mathbf{P}_{h}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
, then we also relate the tangential derivatives with the help of the lift extension defined above as follows:

Scalar Functions ::

Using the extension of a scalar function
\zeta_{h}:\Gamma_{h}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, (
4.13
) and the chain rule we have that the discrete tangential gradient of
z_{h}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
for
x\in\Gamma_{h}
is

(4.25)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}z_{h}(x)=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}z_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))={\mathbf{P}_{h}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H})\nabla_{\Gamma}z_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))={\bm{B}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma}z_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x)),

We also notice that
{\bm{B}_{h}}:T_{x}(\Gamma_{h})\mapsto T_{\pi(x)}(\Gamma)
is invertible; see
Lemma

4.2
, i.e. we have that the map
{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}
on the tangent space
T(\Gamma)
is given by

{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}|_{T_{x}\Gamma}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H})^{-1}({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}}){\mathbf{P}_{h}},

thus for
z_{h}:\Gamma_{h}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and for
x\in\Gamma_{h}
we have that (see also
[
21
]
),

(4.26)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}z_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H})^{-1}({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}})\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}z_{h}(x)={\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}z_{h}(x).

Noticing that the matrix
{\mathbf{G}}=({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}})=({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}}){\mathbf{P}_{h}}
we can easily calculate that
{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}{\bm{B}_{h}}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
and
{\bm{B}_{h}}{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}={\mathbf{P}_{h}}
.

Vector Valued Functions ::

Considering the tangential gradients of vector valued function (
2.4
), we may apply the lift extension component-wise, for each row, and with similar calculations to the scalar case we may get for
\bm{z}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
, after factoring out the common map
{\bm{B}_{h}}
, that

(4.27)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\bm{z}_{h}(x)=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\bm{z}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))=\nabla_{\Gamma}\bm{z}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x)){\bm{B}_{h}}^{t}.

Likewise for the inverse transformation we get

(4.28)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\bm{z}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\bm{z}_{h}(x)({\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1})^{t}.

Furthermore, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{h}=\frac{1}{\mu_{h}}{\bm{B}_{h}}^{t}{\bm{B}_{h}}=\frac{1}{\mu_{h}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{P}_{h}}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H})
; see
[
28
,
21
,
61
]
and
\mathcal{W}_{h}=\frac{1}{\mu_{h}}{\bm{B}_{h}}-{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
, which will be important later on in
Section

4.7
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\Gamma(t)
and
\Gamma_{h}(t)
be as described above. Then for sufficiently small
h
we have the following bounds

(4.29)

\displaystyle||{\bm{B}_{h}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}

\displaystyle\leq c,\qquad\qquad||{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq 1,

\displaystyle||\partial_{h}^{(k)}\mathcal{W}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||\partial_{h}^{(k)}{\mathbf{P}}\mathcal{W}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},\quad||{\mathbf{P}}-\mathcal{Q}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},

(4.30)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\qquad\qquad||{\mathbf{P}}(\partial_{h}^{(k)}\mathcal{Q}_{h}){\mathbf{P}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}

where the superscript
(k)
denotes the
k
-th discrete material derivative.

Proof.

For (
4.29
) recall
[
46
,
27
,
43
,
32
]
. Regarding
(
4.2
) the last two estimates have been shown in
[
54
,
61
]
. The first inequality in (
4.2
) is an immediate consequence of the first inequality in (
4.18
) and the second of the second inequality in (
4.18
).
∎

4.3.1.
Norm equivalence

We use
\sim
to denote the norm equivalence independent of
h
. Recall the following lemma involving equivalence of norms on different surfaces.

Lemma 4.3

(Norm Equivalence)
.

Let
v_{h}\in H^{j}(T),

j\geq 2
for
T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
a face of
\Gamma_{h}
and let

T^{\ell}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{\ell}
be a face of the lifted surface
\Gamma
. Then for
h
small enough we have that following equivalences

(4.31)

\displaystyle||v_{h}^{\ell}||_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}

\displaystyle\sim||v_{h}||_{L^{2}(T)}\sim||\tilde{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\tilde{T})},

\displaystyle||\nabla_{\Gamma}v_{h}^{\ell}||_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}

\displaystyle\sim||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}v_{h}||_{L^{2}(T)}\sim||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}^{(1)}}\tilde{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\tilde{T})},

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{j}v_{h}|_{L^{2}(T)}\leq c\sum_{k=1}^{j}

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\Gamma}^{k}v_{h}^{\ell}|_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})},\qquad|\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}v_{h}^{\ell}|_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}\leq c\sum_{k=1}^{j}|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{k}v_{h}|_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}.

Proof.

The first two set of equivalences can be found in several literature, e.g.
[
27
,
20
]
. Regarding the last two inequalities, see
[
57
, Lemma 3.3]
.
∎

4.4.
Material Velocity of Lifted Surface

In
Section

4.2
we described that the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}(t)
evolves with discrete material velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
. With this in mind, following
[
28
,
27
,
31
]
, the associated material velocity of the
lifted
material points
{\mathbf{y}}(t)
of
\Gamma(t)
are defined as

(4.32)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{v}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}({\mathbf{y}}(t),t)=\dot{{\mathbf{y}}}(t)=({\mathbf{P}}-d\bm{H})({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t){\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t)-d_{t}({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t){\mathbf{n}}({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t)-d({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t){\mathbf{n}}_{t}({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t),

where
{\mathbf{y}}(t)=\pi({\mathbf{x}}_{h}(t),t)
(
2.14
), with
{\mathbf{x}}_{h}(t)\in\Gamma_{h}(t)
. Then, since
d_{t}({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t){\mathbf{n}}({\mathbf{x}}_{h},t)
is just the normal component of the fluid velocity (in our setting it is our normal fluid velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
), and the rest of the terms are tangential, it is clear that
{\mathbf{v}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}-{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}

is tangent
.

So, as before, this
lifted discrete material (fluid) velocity

{\mathbf{v}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
, induces a lifted discrete flow map
\Phi^{\ell}_{t}:[0,T]\times\Gamma(t)
from which we write the pushforward and pullback of
\chi_{h}^{\ell}\in\Gamma(0)
and
\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\in\Gamma(t)
as

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{\ell}\chi_{h}^{\ell}=\chi_{h}^{\ell}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{\ell}\in\Gamma(t)\qquad\phi_{-t}^{\ell}\zeta_{h}^{\ell}=\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\circ\Phi_{t}^{\ell}\in\Gamma(0),

with
\Phi^{\ell}_{t}({\mathbf{x}})=\Phi^{\ell}({\mathbf{x}},t)=\Phi^{h}({\mathbf{x}}^{-\ell},t)^{\ell}=\Phi^{h}_{t}({\mathbf{x}}^{-\ell})^{\ell}
. So, one may also define the
lifted discrete strong normal material derivative
by

(4.33)

\partial^{\circ}_{\ell}\zeta=\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\zeta\circ\Phi_{t}^{\ell}\Big)\circ\Phi_{-t}^{\ell}=\phi_{t}^{\ell}\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\phi_{-t}^{\ell}\zeta\Big),

for a sufficiently smooth discrete function
\zeta:\mathcal{G}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For more details, see also
[
31
]
. Therefore, it is clear that
\partial^{\circ}_{\ell}\zeta=(\partial^{\circ}_{h}\zeta^{-\ell})^{\ell}
. Furthermore, the
transport property
clearly still holds for the lifted material derivative; see
[
28
]
, i.e.

(4.34)

\partial^{\circ}_{\ell}(\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k})^{\ell}(\cdot,t)=0,\quad\text{for }j=1,...,J,

where, as in
Section

4.2
,
\{(\chi_{j,k_{g}}^{k})^{\ell}(\cdot,t)\},

j=1,...,J
is the lifted nodal basis of
(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}(t))^{\ell}.

Now notice, once again, that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
is the Lagrange interpolant of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
onto the finite element space
(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{u}}(t))^{3}
. With that in mind, the following high order bounds have been derived in
[
31
, Lemma 9.22]
or
[
54
, Lemma 5.4]
for the difference between the flow velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
and the lifted discrete flow velocity
{\mathbf{v}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
(
4.32
).

Lemma 4.4
.

For
a=0,1
it holds that

(4.35)

||\partial_{\ell}^{a}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}+h||\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial_{\ell}^{a}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\leq c(h^{k_{g}+1}+h^{k_{u}+1}),

where
c
a positive constant depending on
\mathcal{G}_{T}
but independent of
h,\,t
.

Furthermore, these bounds allow us to relate the two material derivatives
\partial^{\circ},

\partial^{\circ}_{\ell}
in the following way; see
[
31
, Lemma 9.25]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\bm{z}:\mathcal{G}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
(scalar:
n=1
, vector-valued:
n=3
) and let
\partial^{\circ}\bm{z}
and
\partial^{\circ}_{\ell}\bm{z}
exist, then

(4.36)

\displaystyle||\partial^{\circ}\bm{z}-\partial^{\circ}_{\ell}\bm{z}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq c(h^{k_{g}+1}+h^{k_{u}+1})||\bm{z}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))},\qquad\text{for }\bm{z}\in(H^{1}(\Gamma(t)))^{n},

\displaystyle||\nabla_{\Gamma}(\partial^{\circ}\bm{z}-\partial^{\circ}_{\ell}\bm{z})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq c(h^{k_{g}}+h^{k_{u}})||\bm{z}||_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))},\qquad\text{for }\bm{z}\in(H^{2}(\Gamma(t)))^{n},

where
c
a positive constant depending on
\mathcal{G}_{T}
but independent of
h,\,t
.

The vector-valued case is just a natural point-wise extension. For more details on these type of results see
[
31
,
27
,
28
,
54
]
and references therein.

4.5.
Discrete Bilinear Forms and Transport Theorem

Analogously to the continuous case in
Section

2.2
we define similarly for the discrete case the following operators for
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
,

(4.37)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}\mathbf{w}_{h}

\displaystyle:={\mathbf{P}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h},\qquad E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h}):=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}\mathbf{w}_{h}+\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov,t}\mathbf{w}_{h}).

Our discrete finite element methods later on
Section

6.2
relies on the following time-dependent discrete bilinear forms and inertia terms.

Definition 4.1
.

For
\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h},\mathbf{z}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
,
q_{h},\xi_{h}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
we define the following bilinear forms:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(t;\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}):=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}\,d\sigma_{h},\qquad\ \ {\mathbf{g}}_{h}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N};\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}):=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\!\!\!\!\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma_{h}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N})\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}\,d\sigma_{h},

\displaystyle a_{S,h}(t;\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}):=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h}):E_{h}(\mathbf{v}_{h})\,d\sigma_{h},\quad

\displaystyle a_{h}(t;\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}):=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\!\!\!\!E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h}):E_{h}(\mathbf{v}_{h})\,d\sigma_{h}+\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\!\!\!\!\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}\,d\sigma_{h},
