We now want to approximate each term on the right-hand side appropriately. This will be possible with the help of the newly established bounds in
Lemmas

7.12
,
7.13
and
7.14
. For simplicity and brevity (compared to
Sections

7.1
and
7.2
) we assume that our constant
C
depends on the on the regularity
2
. Then, a simple use of Young’s inequality (similarly to
Lemma

7.8
) gives us the following bounds :

\bullet
Using
Lemma

7.13
and Ritz-Stokes bounds (
5.4
) we derive similarly to
Lemma

7.8

\displaystyle|\sum_{i=1}^{3}\textsc{Err}_{i}^{C}(\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n}))|\leq c\big((\Delta t)^{2}+h^{\widehat{r}_{u}+1}\big)\Big(\int_{t_{n-1}}^{t^{n}}||\partial^{\circ}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+||\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\,dt\Big)

\displaystyle+C\Delta t\big(h^{2r_{u}}+h^{2k_{pr}+2}+h^{2k_{\lambda}+2})+\frac{1}{6\Delta t}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}.

\bullet
For interpolation error, once again, we just recall
Lemma

7.12
to see that

\displaystyle|\textsc{Err}_{1}^{I}(\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n}))|\leq C\Delta t(h^{2r_{u}}+h^{2k_{pr}+2}+h^{2k_{\lambda}+2})+\frac{1}{6\Delta t}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}.

\bullet
For convective error we use the bounds in
Lemma

7.14
, where applying Young’s inequality consecutively and the inverse inequality appropriately, yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n}))\leq C\Delta t\big(||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n-1})}^{2}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}||_{a_{h}}^{2}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{12\Delta t}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle+ch^{-2}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n-1})}^{2}\Delta t||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}^{2}+\frac{1}{12\Delta t}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle c\frac{1}{\Delta t}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n-1})}||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}\Delta t\big(||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}||_{a_{h}}^{2}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{2}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{a_{h}}^{2}.

\bullet
Finally , regarding the second to last term in (
7.72
) a simple Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n},\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n}))\Big)|\leq\Delta t||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}+\frac{1}{6\Delta t}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}.

Now, combining all the bounds above into (
7.72
), applying the applying the velocity error estimates in (
7.57
), (
7.58
), using again the fact that by (
6.8
)
\frac{1}{\Delta t}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}=\Delta t||\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}=\Delta t{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n},\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n})
and employing a kickback argument for this term and
\frac{1}{2}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}-\underline{\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}}(t_{n})||_{a_{h}}^{2}
, we obtain the following, after summing for
k=1,...,n

\displaystyle\Delta t\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{k},\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{k})+||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}^{2}\leq C_{1}(C,\epsilon)\Big(\big(\Delta t\big)^{2}+h^{2r_{u}}+h^{2k_{pr}+2}+h^{2k_{\lambda}+2}\Big)+\epsilon\Delta t||\sigma_{\{p,\lambda\}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle+C\big(\frac{1}{\Delta t}+h^{-2}\big)\big((\Delta t)^{2}+h^{2r_{u}}+h^{2k_{pr}+2}+h^{2k_{\lambda}+2}\big)\Delta t\sum_{k=1}^{n}\big(||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}||_{a_{h}}^{2}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}^{2}\big),

since
\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{0}=0
due to the choice of the initial condition. Applying once again (
7.57
), (
7.58
) and recalling the fact that
k_{g}\geq 2
yields our assertion.
∎

Lemma 7.16
.

Assume
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
,
k_{g}\geq 2
, and let the regularity
2
hold with time-step
\Delta t\leq ch
. Let
({\mathbf{u}},\{p,\lambda\})
be the solution of (
eNSWc
) and let
(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{k},\{p_{h}^{k},\lambda_{h}^{k}\})
,
k=1,...,n
be the discrete solutions of (
eNSWch
), with initial condition
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{0}=\mathcal{R}_{h}^{b}{\mathbf{u}}^{0}
. Then, the following holds for
1\leq n\leq N

(7.73)

\Delta t\sum_{k=1}^{n}||\sigma_{\{p,\lambda\}}^{k}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{k})\times L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{k})}^{2}\leq C(1+\frac{h^{2m}}{\Delta t})\Big(\big(\Delta t\big)^{2}+h^{2m}\Big),

where
m=min\{r_{u},k_{pr}+1,k_{\lambda}+1\}
,
r_{u}=min\{k_{u},k_{g}-1\}
, with constant
C>0
depending on the regularity
2
.

Proof.

The proof follows almost identically to
Lemma

7.9
, where now we use the auxiliary result
Lemma

7.15
and employ the
L^{2}\times L^{2}

inf-sup
(
6.40
) instead. So, recall that

(7.74)

\displaystyle||\{\sigma_{p}^{n},\sigma_{\lambda}^{n}\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})\times L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}\leq\sup_{\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n}}\frac{b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n},\{\sigma_{p}^{n},\sigma_{\lambda}^{n}\})}{||\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}||_{a_{h}}}.

Considering the error equation (
EQc
), the calculations in (
7.70
), (
6.8
) and (
6.9
), and the estimates in
Lemmas

7.12
,
7.13
and
7.14
, then solving for
b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n},\{\sigma_{p}^{n},\sigma_{\lambda}^{n}\})
yields

(7.75)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle||\{\sigma_{p}^{n},\sigma_{\lambda}^{n}\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})\times L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}\leq||\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}+||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}+c||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}

\displaystyle+c(\Delta t+h^{m})\sup_{t\in I_{n}}\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{0}\big(||(\partial^{\circ})^{j}{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}+||(\partial^{\circ})^{j}p||_{H^{k_{pr}+1}(\Gamma(t))}+||(\partial^{\circ})^{j}\lambda||_{H^{k_{\lambda}+1}(\Gamma(t))}\big)\Big\}

\displaystyle+c(\sqrt{\Delta t}+\frac{h^{\widehat{r}_{u}+1}}{\sqrt{\Delta t}})\Big(\int_{t_{n-1}}^{t^{n}}||\partial^{\circ}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\,dt\Big)^{1/2}+||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n-1})}^{1/2}||\sigma_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{n-1}||_{a_{h}}^{1/2}||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}

\displaystyle+||{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{W^{2,\infty}(\Gamma^{n})}\big(||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n-1})}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}||_{a_{h}}+||{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{n}||_{a_{h}}\big),

with
m=min\{r_{u},k_{pr}+1,k_{\lambda}+1\}
,
r_{u}=min\{k_{u},k_{g}-1\}
, where we used the fact that
||\cdot||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}\leq||\cdot||_{a_{h}}
in (
7.68
) and
||\cdot||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}\leq ch^{-1}||\cdot||_{a_{h}}
(
4.52
) in (
7.13
) for the test function.

Now, squaring (
7.75
) and applying the operator
\Delta t\sum_{k=1}^{n}
(i.e. multiplying
\Delta t
and summing over
k=1,...,n
), it is clear that if we apply the velocity error convergence results (
7.57
), (
7.58
) and the afore-mentioned auxiliary estimate (
7.69
) along with a kickback for the last pressure term in (
7.69
) (assumming
\epsilon>0
is small enough, i.e.
\epsilon=1/2
) yields our assertion.
∎

Finally, by applying the triangle inequality on the decomposition (
7.3
), the above
Lemma

7.16
and the Ritz-Stokes interpolation error (
5.4
) we obtain the final
error pressure result
.

Theorem 7.17

(Pressure error bounds II)
.

Under the regularity assumption
2
and
Lemma

7.16
the following pressure error estimates holds for
1\leq n\leq N

(7.76)

\Delta t\sum_{k=1}^{n}||\{{\mathbf{e}}_{p}^{k},{\mathbf{e}}_{\lambda}^{k}\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{k})\times L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{k})}^{2}\leq C(1+\frac{h^{2m}}{\Delta t})\Big(\big(\Delta t\big)^{2}+h^{2m}\Big),

where
m=min\{r_{u},k_{pr}+1,k_{\lambda}+1\}
,
r_{u}=min\{k_{u},k_{g}-1\}
, with positive constant
C
depending on the regularity
2
.

8.
Numerical results

Now we present some numerical results to validate our theoretical conclusions. We start in
Section

8.1
with a general setup of our experiments, defining the parameters, the formulations used, and specifying the notation.
In
Section

8.2
we examine and test the choice of the finite element space
\Lambda_{h}
, i.e. the choice of the two parameters
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
and
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
.
Finally, in
Section

8.3
we compare approximation schemes of (
eNSW
), see (
LMM
) below, to a penalty-based formulation (
PM
) for the tangential Navier-Stokes on an evolving surface, with an appropriate penalty parameter; see
[
72
,
69
,
55
]
.

8.1.
Setup

As mentioned in
Remark

6.1
, in practice we do not implement the numerical schemes (
eNSWdh
), (
eNSWch
) that are based on the simplified weak formulations (
eNSWd
), (
eNSWc
) according to
Remark

3.2
. Instead, we have
{\bm{\eta}}\neq 0
; see (
3.11
), and therefore want to consider finite element approximations based on the weak formulations (
eNSW
); recall that (
eNSW
) represents two problems depending on the choice of
c^{(\cdot)}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
. So, we define our numerical schemes (
LMM
) (labeled as LMM: Lagrange Multiplier Method since we make use of a Lagrange multiplier for the normal velocity restriction) naturally as follows:

(LMM):

Given
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{0}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{0,div}
and appropriate approximation for the data
{\bm{f}}_{h},\,{\eta}_{h}^{n}
, for
n=0,1,...,N
find
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n}
,
\{p_{h}^{n},\lambda_{h}^{n}\}\in Q_{h}^{n}\times\Lambda_{h}^{n}
such that

(LMM)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\Delta t}\Big({\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})-{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n-1})\Big)-{\mathbf{g}}_{h}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N};\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})+a_{S,h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})\\
+c_{h}^{(\cdot)}(\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(\cdot,t_{n}),\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})+b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n},\{p_{h}^{n},\lambda_{h}^{n}\})={\mathbf{m}}_{h}({\bm{f}}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})+{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1},\underline{\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}}(\cdot,t^{n-1})),\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \!b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},\{q_{h}^{n},\xi_{h}^{n}\})=m_{h}(\bm{\eta}_{h}^{n},\{q_{h}^{n},\xi_{h}^{n}\}),\end{cases}

for all
\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n-1}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n-1}
and
\{q_{h}^{n},\xi_{h}^{n}\}\in Q_{h}^{n}\times\Lambda_{h}^{n}
.
Here we used (
6.8
), compared to (
eNSWdh
), (
eNSWch
), to define our schemes and furthermore, now,
c_{h}^{(\cdot)}(\bullet,\bullet,\bullet)
denotes the skew-symmetrized type discretizations of
c^{(\cdot)}
as described in (
3.13
) and (
3.3
) (recall also
Definition

4.1
used in the schemes (
eNSWdh
), (
eNSWch
) analyzed in
Section

7
):

\displaystyle c_{h}(\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n});\mathbf{u}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}):=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}((\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}})\mathbf{u}_{h})\mathbf{v}_{h}\,d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}((\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}})\mathbf{v}_{h})\mathbf{u}_{h}\,d\sigma_{h}\Big)

(8.1)

\displaystyle\hskip 79.66771pt-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\eta_{h,2}\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}\,d\sigma_{h},

\displaystyle c_{h}^{cov}(\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n});\mathbf{u}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}):=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Gamma_{h}}((\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov})\mathbf{u}_{h})\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma_{h}}((\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov})\mathbf{v}_{h})\cdot\mathbf{u}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}(\mathbf{u}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n})\cdot\bm{H}_{h}\mathbf{v}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}(\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(t_{n})\cdot\bm{H}_{h}\mathbf{u}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}\Big)

(8.2)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\eta_{h,1}\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\cdot\bm{H}_{h}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}\,d\sigma_{h}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\eta_{h,2}\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\cdot{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}\,d\sigma_{h}.

It is clear that for (
LMM
), we also require a sufficiently accurate approximation of the data
\bm{\eta}
(
3.11
) in the right-hand side of the second equation in (
eNSW
) (i.e.
\bm{\eta}_{h}
), e.g.

(8.3)

\bm{\eta}_{h}=({\eta}_{h,1},{\eta}_{h,2})=(-\kappa_{h}V_{\Gamma_{h}},V_{\Gamma_{h}}),

where
V_{\Gamma_{h}}
is an interpolation of
V_{\Gamma}
similar to
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N}
in (
4.7
) and
\kappa_{h}=tr(\bm{H}_{h})
; see also
Remark

8.1
. Clearly, the expression (
8.1
) is easier to implement than (
8.2
), since it does not require an approximation for either the mean curvature
\bm{H}_{h}
or
\eta_{h,1}
. This formulation still yields the expected relative errors that we proved in
Section

7
for the numerical schemes (
eNSWdh
), (
eNSWch
), i.e. for
\bm{\eta}=0
.
Lastly, recall that for nodal test functions
\underline{\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\chi_{j}^{n}}(\cdot,t^{n-1})=0
; see (
6.6
), and therefore for the implementation of (
LMM
) the last term in the first equations is ignored.

Remark 8.1
.

We can also consider a new scheme by rewriting the second equation of (
eNSW
) with the help of the integration by parts formula (
2.11
) to obtain

\int_{\Gamma(t)}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}\,q\,d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\xi\,{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\xi\,V_{\Gamma}\,d\sigma,

and discretize the above expression instead. This means that we no longer need to approximate the inhomogeneous term
\eta_{1}
(
\eta_{h,1}=-\kappa_{h}V_{\Gamma_{h}}
; see (
8.3
)), so no approximation of a geometric quantity is needed anymore, unless we discretize the convective term (
3.3
); see (
8.2
) above. This also might be a reason to prefer to discretize (
LMM
) with
c_{h}
. We note that we observed similar convergence results.

The numerical results were implemented using the
Firedrake
package
[
42
]
, where the linear systems were solved using GMRES with the help of built in multigrid preconditioners. The finite element approximations were carried out on sequences of regular bisection mesh-refinements. As noted in
Remark

6.2
, for (
eNSWch
) we consider
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
, whilst for (
eNSWdh
) we mainly set
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
. We also observed that using the (
eNSWdh
) scheme for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
, appeared to
produce optimal convergence results
, although we were unable to find such theoretical results.

We, finally, introduce the following notation for the error measure:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{L^{2}(a_{h})}

\displaystyle:=\Big(\Delta t\sum_{n=1}^{N}||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}({\mathbf{u}}^{n}-\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}\Big)^{1/2},\quad{\mathbf{e}}_{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}}^{L^{\infty}({L^{2}})}:=\max_{0\leq n\leq N}||\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma_{h}}({\mathbf{u}}^{n}-\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{L^{2}(H^{1})}

\displaystyle:=\Big(\Delta t\sum_{n=1}^{N}||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}({\mathbf{u}}^{n}-\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}\Big)^{1/2},\quad{\mathbf{e}}_{p}^{L^{2}({L^{2}})}:=\Big(\Delta t\sum_{n=1}^{N}||p^{n}-p_{h}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}^{2}\Big)^{1/2},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{L^{\infty}({L^{2}})}

\displaystyle:=\max_{0\leq n\leq N}||{\mathbf{u}}^{n}-\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})},\qquad\qquad\ \ {\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{u}}}^{L^{\infty}({L^{2}})}:=\max_{0\leq n\leq N}||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}({\mathbf{u}}^{n}-\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}}^{L^{\infty}({L^{2}})}

\displaystyle:=\max_{0\leq n\leq N}||\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}-V_{\Gamma}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}.

8.2.
Example 1: Moving sphere

Similar to
[
69
]
, this example concerns a sphere translating to the right. The evolving surface
\Gamma(t)
is defined via a level set function as

(8.4)

D({\mathbf{x}},t)=(x_{1}-0.2t)^{2}+(x_{2})^{2}+(x_{3})^{2}-1,\qquad\Gamma(t)=\{{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ |\ D({\mathbf{x}},t)=0\}.

with final time
T=2
. The normal velocity is given by
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}=\frac{-\partial_{t}D}{||\nabla D||}\frac{\nabla D}{||\nabla D||}
=
V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
, cf.
[
27
]
. Using the parameterization

(8.5)

F(s,t)=(s_{1}+0.2t,s_{2},s_{3}),\qquad p\in\mathcal{S},

such that
\Gamma(t)=F(\mathcal{S},t)
, the evolving triangulated surface is constructed by creating an initial mesh
\mathcal{S}
of the sphere and then evolving the nodes via
F
. Hence the vertices evolve with a velocity
{\mathbf{w}}=\partial_{t}F(s,t)=(0.2t,0,0)^{T}
and we consider the tangential part of
{\mathbf{w}}
as an arbitrary tangential velocity (ALE-scheme), cf.
[
34
]
. The viscosity parameter is
\mu=1/2
and the density distributionis
\rho=1
. Now, instead of using the example in
[
69
]
, our solution is defined as

(8.6)

{\mathbf{u}}_{T}=\textbf{curl}_{\Gamma}\psi\quad\text{with}\quad\psi=(x_{1}-0.2tx_{3})x_{2}-2t,\qquad p=(x_{1}-0.2t)x_{2}+x_{3},

where
\textbf{curl}_{\Gamma}={\mathbf{n}}\times\nabla_{\Gamma}
, which by
[
71
]
we know is tangential. Moreover
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}_{T}=0
, while
p\in L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma(t))
. Despite not including the plot here, our experiments, would give the
exact same convergence rates
for the solution of
[
69
, Section 5]
. The data on the right-hand side
({\bm{f}}_{h}
,
\bm{\eta}_{h})
, see (
LMM
),
is an interpolation of the known smooth data.

We compare the primitive solutions with respect to the parameter choice
k_{\lambda}
. First, we examine the case where
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}=2}
, for which the polynomial degree of the Lagrange multiplier space
\Lambda_{h}
is equal to the degree of the velocity space
{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
. In this case, we use the formulation (
eNSWdh
) (i.e. (
LMM
) with
c_{h}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
, see
Section

8.1
) and employ
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{2}
–
\mathrm{P}_{1}
–
\mathrm{P}_{2}

Taylor-Hood iso-parametric finite elements
, so
k_{g}=k_{u}=2
. Second, we examine the
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1=1}
case and utilize the formulation (
eNSWch
) (i.e. (
LMM
) with
c_{h}^{cov}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
) according to the error analysis in
Section

7
. So, we employ
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{2}
–
\mathrm{P}_{1}
–
\mathrm{P}_{1}

Taylor-Hood super-parametric surface finite elements
, i.e. with surface order approximation
k_{g}=3
. This reflects the limiting geometric factor
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{k_{g}-1})
appearing in
Theorems

7.7
and
7.17
.

Figure 1
.
Moving-Right Sphere | Velocity - Pressure Errors
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{L^{\infty}(a_{h})}
,
{\mathbf{e}}_{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}}^{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}
,
{\mathbf{e}}_{p}^{L^{2}(L^{2})}
| Case 1:
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
corresponds to geometric approximation
k_{g}=k_{u}=2
| Case 2:
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
correspond to geometric approximation
k_{g}=k_{u}+1=3
.

Starting with an initial mesh size of
h_{0}=0.66
and time-step
\Delta t_{0}=0.5
we consider a series of regular bisection mesh-refinements, such that the spatial mesh size is halved while the temporal step is reduced by a factor of four, so that
\Delta t\sim h^{2}
. As mentioned, in this particular example we notice that the spatial error dominates the behavior of our numerical approximations.

In
Fig.

1
we observe optimal rates for all our quantities. In particular for the energy and pressure estimates
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{L^{2}(a_{h})}
,
e_{p}^{L^{2}(L^{2})}
and we observe second order convergence, as expected from our theoretical results
Theorems

7.7
,
7.10
and
7.17
. Moreover, we notice reduced rate,
{\mathcal{O}}(h)
, for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{L^{2}(H^{1})}
, in the
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
case, in line with the worse
H^{1}
-coercivity estimate (
4.52
). This is not the case for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
(as expected); see the improved
H^{1}
-coercivity estimate (
4.70
).

The
L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}
velocity errors are presented in
Fig.

2
. First, we report expected
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{2})
convergence rates for the normal approximation
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}}^{L^{\infty}({L^{2}})}
when
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
and better than expected
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{3})
for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
; see
[
32
, Theorem 6.16]
where
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{2.5})

error bounds were established for the Stokes problem on stationary surfaces (or
Lemma

5.3
) and
[
33
]
where it was observed numerically for the unsteady Navier-Stokes equations on stationary surfaces.

Figure 2
.
Moving-Right Sphere | Velocity Errors
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{u}}}^{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}
,
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{u}}}^{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}
,
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}}^{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}
| Case 1:
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
corresponds to geometric approximation
k_{g}=k_{u}=2
| Case 2:
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
correspond to geometric approximation
k_{g}=k_{u}+1=3
.

We also observe optimal convergence for the full
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}
errors in the
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
case; see
Theorem

7.7
, but notice higher than expected convergence rates
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{2.5})
for the tangent part
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{u}}}^{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}
.
Similarly, we observe
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{2.5})
convergence in both full and tangential
L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}
velocity errors for the
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
case (it is expected that the full and tangential velocity errors would coincide in this case, since the normal velocity error is
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{3})
; see
[
32
, Theorem 6.14, Theorem 6.16]
). Although this behavior is expected for the stationary surface Stokes (or unsteady Navier-Stokes) case, see
[
32
, Theorem 6.14, Theorem 6.16]
or
[
33
]
(in actuality in that case we expect an increase of one full order
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{3})
and not half order
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{2.5})
), it is not clear that this should be the case in the evolving setting. Indeed, the Ritz-Stokes estimates for the material derivative in
Lemmas

5.8
and
5.9
do not indicate any increase in convergence. Therefore, we believe that this might be indicative of the material derivative Ritz-Stokes estimates not being sharp, e.g. (
5.42
) where we only prove an
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\widehat{r}_{u}})={\mathcal{O}}(h^{2})
convergence. This is also speculated in
Remarks

5.5
and
5.4
, where we believe that it should be possible to show the half-order increase, at least in the geometric part of the error.

Lastly, we also note a surprising super-convergence result for the
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
case, which was also observed in
[
32
, Section 7.3]
, namely, that for affine finite element (
k_{g}=1
) we observe almost
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{2})
convergence rates across all of our quantities
Fig.

3
.

About the choice of the approximation space
\Lambda_{h}
, i.e. the choice of
k_{\lambda}
: Letting
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
, while we do obtain optimal results even when using
iso-parametric surface finite elements
, this comes at the expense of stability of the system, since for this choice the condition of the corresponding systems of equations scales much worse w.r.t. the mesh parameter
h
; for more details we refer to
[
36
]
.

Figure 3
.
Moving-Right Sphere | Velocity - Pressure Errors | Flat Case:
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}=2
corresponds to geometric approximation
k_{g}=1
.

8.3.
Example 2: Comparison Oscillating Sphere

In this example, we perform a simple comparison test for our numerical scheme (
LMM
) based on the weak formulation (
eNSc
) (i.e.
c^{(\cdot)}=c^{cov}
; see also (
eNSWch
) which we analyzed) when
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
and a penalty-based numerical scheme for the tangential Navier-Stokes on a passively evolving surfaces with prescribed velocity
[
66
]
; see (
PM
) below. The latter scheme was considered and studied extensively in
[
69
]
where the authors used unfitted finite elements (TraceFem).
In our case, we present a naive numerical approximation (
PM
) (PM: Penalty Method) using fitted finite elements (ESFEM). Similar schemes were studied for the Stokes equations on stationary surfaces
[
72
]
, where a comparison to the Lagrange-based approach was carried out in
[
32
,
33
]
.
Furthermore, a scheme more similar to ours (
LMM
) was also studied numerically in
[
55
]
, where the authors, this time, used a penalty-based approach to constraint the normal velocity. However, since they used a mesh regularization approach, and did not perform an error analysis, we decided against comparing it to our method. The penalty scheme we compare it with, instead, is the following:

(PM):

Given
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{0}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{0,div}
and appropriate approximation for the data
{\bm{f}}_{h},\,{\eta}_{h}^{n}
, for
n=0,1,...,N
find
\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n}
,
\{p_{h}^{n},\lambda_{h}^{n}\}\in Q_{h}^{n}\times\Lambda_{h}^{n}
such that

(PM)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\Delta t}\Big({\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},{\mathbf{P}}_{h}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})-{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1},{\mathbf{P}}_{h}^{n-1}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n-1})\Big)-{\mathbf{g}}_{h}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma_{h}}^{N};\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},{\mathbf{P}}_{h}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})+a_{S,h}^{T}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})\\
\quad+c_{h}^{cov}(\underline{\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1}}(\cdot,t_{n}),\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})+b_{h}(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n},p_{h}^{n})={\mathbf{m}}_{h}({\bm{f}}_{h}^{n},{\mathbf{P}}_{h}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n})+{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1},{\mathbf{P}}_{h}^{n-1}\underline{\partial^{\circ}_{h,\tau}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}}(\cdot,t_{n-1})),\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ \ \!b_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n},q_{h}^{n})=m_{h}({\eta}_{h}^{n},q_{h}^{n}).\end{cases}

for all
\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n}
and
\mathbf{v}_{h}^{n-1}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{n-1}
and
\{q_{h}^{n},\xi_{h}^{n}\}\in Q_{h}^{n}\times\Lambda_{h}^{n}
, where
a_{S,h}^{T}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the bilinear form
\hat{a}_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)
in
[
69
, Eq. (4.1)]
, without the added stabilization term (in our setting
w_{N}=V_{\Gamma}
). Therefore, hidden also in this bilinear form is the penalty term
\tau{\mathbf{m}}_{h}(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\cdot\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}^{n},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}^{n})
which corresponds to the tangentiality condition
{\mathbf{u}}^{n}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}=0
. According to
[
69
]
, to obtain optimal convergence we consider a higher-order approximation for the normal
\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}
, than the usual
{\mathbf{n}}_{h}
, i.e.
||{\mathbf{n}}-\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}
, and also choose the penalty term appropriately, e.g.
\tau=\frac{h^{-2}}{2}
. For the construction of such normal
\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}
see
[
43
, Remark 3.3]
for further details. Finally, notice that the source terms
{\bm{f}}_{h}^{n}
,
{\eta}_{h}^{n}
are different from the source terms in (
LMM
). For instance, now,
{\eta}_{h}=\eta_{h,1}
and so it does not include the term
\eta_{h,2}=V_{\Gamma_{h}}
; see (
8.3
), since we approximate the tangential Navier-Stokes on a passively evolving surface instead.

Figure 4
.
Oscillating Sphere | Errors | (
LMM
): {
k_{u}=2,\,k_{pr}=k_{\lambda}=1,\,k_{g}=3
}, (
PM
): {
k_{u}=2,\,k_{pr}=1,\,k_{g}=2,\,\tau\sim h^{-2},\,|\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}-{\mathbf{n}}|\sim h^{k_{g}+1}\sim h^{3}
}

To compare the two methods, we consider an oscillating sphere
\mathcal{S}
given by

(8.7)

d({\mathbf{x}},t)=||{\mathbf{x}}||_{2}^{2}-r(t),\qquad\text{with }r(t)=1+\frac{1}{4}sin(2\pi t),

where
T=1
the final time, and therefore the surface normal velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}
is given by
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}=V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
, where
V_{\Gamma}(t)=r^{\prime}(t)=\frac{\pi}{2}cos(2\pi t)
. We also describe an exact tangential solution as

(8.8)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}=\textbf{curl}_{\Gamma}\psi,

\displaystyle\text{with}\ \psi=(1-2t)\frac{1}{2\pi}\cos(2\pi x_{1})\cos(2\pi x_{2})\cos(2\pi x_{3}),

\displaystyle p=\sin(\pi x_{1})\sin(2\pi x_{2})\sin(2\pi x_{3}).

where
\textbf{curl}_{\Gamma}={\mathbf{n}}\times\nabla_{\Gamma}
, which by
[
71
]
we know it is tangential and moreover
p\in L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma(t))
. The data then on the right-hand side of both (
LMM
) and (
PM
), can be calculated numerically with the help of the exact solution as interpolation of the smooth data.

For (
LMM
) we use
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{2}
–
\mathrm{P}_{1}
–
\mathrm{P}_{1}

Taylor-Hood

super-parametric finite elements
, with surface approximation of order
k_{g}=3
. While for (
PM
) we use
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{2}
–
\mathrm{P}_{1}

Taylor-Hood
iso-parametric surface finite elements with surface order approximation
k_{g}=2
, while as noted before, for the penalty parameter we use
\tau=\frac{h^{-2}}{2}
and the improved normal
\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}
.

We set the viscosity parameter
\mu=2\times 10^{-2}
and the density distribution
\rho=1
, for both cases, and use a sufficiently accurate quadrature rule, starting with an initial mesh size of
h_{0}=0.62
and a time step
\Delta t_{0}=0.5
and perform a series of regular bisection mesh-refinements, such that the spatial refinement is halved while the temporal refinement is reduced by a factor of four, ensuring that
\Delta t\sim h^{2}
. That, then, allows us to obtain optimal convergence; see
Theorems

7.7
and
7.17
.

Now,
Fig.

4
depicts the experimental order of convergence for both (
LMM
) and (
PM
). We observe the same optimal convergence for all our quantities. In particular, the only difference (in magnitude of error to precise) appears in the normal approximations, which was expected. Indeed, the normal convergence rate for (
PM
) aligns with the known literature
[
72
,
32
,
33
]
on stationary surfaces, while we observe an improved rate
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{3})
for (
LMM
)
[
32
,
33
]
(expected an
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{2})
for
k_{\lambda}=k_{g}-1
). For the normal approximation, we want to note, once again, that (
LMM
) compares the quantity
\max_{0\leq n\leq N}||\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}-V_{\Gamma}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}
, while the penalty method (
PM
) the quantity
\max_{0\leq n\leq N}||\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}\cdot\tilde{{\mathbf{n}}}_{h}^{n}-0||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}^{n})}
.

((a))

((b))

((c))

((d))

Figure 5
.
Oscillating Sphere | Tangent Velocity
{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}
at different times-steps for
\mathbf{k_{g}=3}
,
\mathbf{k_{u}=2}
,
\mathbf{k_{pr}=k_{\lambda}=1}
, with mesh size
h=0.09
and
\Delta t=2.5\times 10^{-2}
. The length and direction of the arrows depict the strength and orientation of the current.

9.
Brief Summary and Outlook

Brief Summary:
We addressed the numerical study of the evolving surface Navier–Stokes equations with prescribed normal velocity. Using Taylor–Hood surface finite elements we developed two fully time-discrete backwards Euler discretizations schemes, see
Section

6.2
, depending on the choice of the convective term
c^{(\cdot)}(\bullet;\bullet,\bullet)
and approximation of the Lagrange multiplier involving the normal velocity constraint
\lambda_{h}
. In order to simplify our calculations, we developed our numerical analysis
based on the discretizations of the solenoidal formulations introduced in
Section

3.3
; see
Remark

6.1
also. For these simplified schemes in
Sections

6.4
and
7
, we established well-posedness as well as optimal
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
and
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{H^{-1}_{h}}
(or
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L_{2}}
) error bounds in
Section

7
, for the velocity and pressures, respectively (again depending on the choice of the discretization analyzed and approximation space of
\lambda_{h}
). To achieve this, we used two important ideas. First, we introduced a new surface Ritz-Stokes projection, presented in
Section

5
, along with appropriate interpolation bounds. Second, we proposed a novel
Leray time-projection
(
6.17
) to ensure weakly divergence conformity for our discrete velocity
solution at two different time-steps (surfaces) and therefore reduce, in a sense, our analysis to that of the stationary case
[
33
]
. That is, proving an auxiliary bound for the approximation of the time-derivative (material derivative) in a weaker dual norm, with the help of an inverse surface Stokes operator, to establish the pressure bounds. Lastly, we also provided numerical results to accompany and validate our theoretical results; see
Section

8
.

Outlook:
Let us briefly discuss some open problems and/or extensions, regarding the numerical study of the evolving surface Navier-Stokes equation (
eNSEL
):

•

In order to present a complete analysis, i.e. prove also an improved
L^{2}_{L^{2}}
(or
L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}
)-norm velocity error bounds, in accordance with our numerical investigation in
Section

8.2
, it seems one would need to improve the material derivative estimates of the surface Ritz-Stokes projection in
Lemma

5.8
; see
Remarks

5.4
and
5.5
for more detailed discussion where we also proved some initial improvements. In general, though, the issue stems from the fact that the material derivative is no longer tangential
\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\neq 0
even if the velocity is
{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=0
.

•

In
[
69
]
an Eulerian finite element method was studied for the tangential surface Navier-Stokes on a passively evolving surface (TSNSE) using Trace-FEM. Based on this, in the numerical experiment in
Section

8.3
, we introduced a new naive penalty approximation scheme (
Eq.

PM
) for (TSNSE) using evolving surface finite elements (ESFEM) instead. Following our analysis in this paper, we believe that it should be possible to prove stability and error bounds for
Eq.

PM
(or similar schemes), since then the analysis simply reduces to finding an appropriate Ritz-Stokes projection estimate
Lemmas

5.3
and
5.8
.

•

The introduction of the
Leray time-projection
(
6.17
) enables us, in a sense, to reduce the numerical analysis of the evolving surface Navier-Stokes equation to the analysis of the stationary case one
[
33
]
. Since the latter follows the arguments and ideas of standard NS equations on bulk domains (adapted to surfaces) e.g.
[
51
,
37
,
19
]
, we believe that results presented in the bulk domain case, e.g. grad-div stabilization
[
19
,
18
]
or other stabilization schemes
[
36
,
51
]
, should be easily accessible to evolving surfaces.

Appendix A
Differential operators

In this appendix, we focus on the properties of surface differential operators. We assume that the surface
\Gamma
is smooth enough. All smooth extensions will be omitted for clarity reasons, i.e.
\partial_{i}f^{e}=\partial_{i}f
. Considering two vectors
{\mathbf{a}},\,{\mathbf{b}}\,\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we use the Einstein summation convention, that is,
{\mathbf{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{b}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}b_{i}=a_{i}b_{i}
. We also introduce the following abbreviation for the i-th partial derivative,
\partial_{i}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
. Then

(\nabla{\mathbf{u}})_{ij}=\partial_{j}(u_{i}),\ 1\leq i,\,j\leq 3
.

Using these notation, we can express the surface differential operators for suitable scalar
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

functions and vector-valued functions
{\mathbf{v}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
as

(A.1)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\Gamma}f)_{i}=P_{ij}\partial_{j}f,\qquad\quad(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}})_{ij}=\partial_{k}v_{i}P_{kj},\qquad\quad(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}({\mathbf{v}}))_{ij}=P_{jk}\partial_{k}v_{l}P_{li},

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{v}})=(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}({\mathbf{v}}))_{ii}=P_{ik}\partial_{k}v_{l}P_{li}=\partial_{k}v_{i}P_{ki},

where
({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})_{ij}=P_{ij}
the orthogonal projection defined in
Section

2.1
. The following relations also hold:

(A.2)

\displaystyle P_{ij}=\delta_{ij}-n_{i}n_{j},\quad P_{ij}n_{j}=0,\quad H_{ij}=\partial_{i}(n_{j})=\partial_{j}(n_{i})

\displaystyle\partial_{k}{P_{ij}}=-n_{i}H_{kj}-n_{j}H_{ki},\quad P_{ik}H_{kj}=H_{ij}.

Many of the results presented here can be found in
[
49
,
63
]
.

Lemma A.1

(Identities)
.

For vector-valued functions
{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}\in C^{1}(\Gamma)^{3}
, where
{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}_{T}+{\mathbf{v}}_{n}
with
{\mathbf{v}}_{n}=v_{N}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
, the following relationships hold:

(A.3)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}})=\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}^{t}{\mathbf{v}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}^{t}{\mathbf{w}}

(A.4)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}+{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})^{t}-{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\bm{H}{\mathbf{v}})^{t},

(A.5)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}+\bm{H}v_{N}.

Proof.

Eq. (
A.3
) is easy to see, since

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}})=P_{ij}\partial_{j}(w_{k}v_{k})=P_{ij}(\partial_{j}w_{k})v_{k}+(P_{ij}\partial_{j}v_{k})w_{k}.

To prove (
A.4
) we notice that

(A.6)

\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}+{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{t}\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{v}}_{T})+{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{t}\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{v}}_{n}).

We keep the first term unchanged and concentrate on the last two terms. So we have

(A.7)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{t}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}

\displaystyle=({\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{t}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{v}})_{ij}=n_{i}n_{l}\partial_{k}\big(({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{v}})_{l}\big)P_{kj}=n_{i}n_{l}\Big(\partial_{k}(P_{lm})v_{m}+P_{lm}\partial_{k}(v_{m})\Big)P_{kj}

\displaystyle=n_{i}n_{l}\big(-H_{km}n_{l}v_{m}-H_{kl}n_{m}v_{m}\big)P_{kj}=-H_{jm}v_{m}n_{i}=-{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\bm{H}{\mathbf{u}})^{t},

where we have used the relations in (
A.2
). Now considering that
{\mathbf{v}}_{n}=({\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=v_{N}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
, we may also obtain the following

(A.8)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{t}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}_{n}

\displaystyle=n_{i}n_{l}\partial_{k}\big(v_{N}n_{l}\big)P_{kj}=n_{i}n_{l}\partial_{k}(v_{N})n_{l}P_{kj}+\underbrace{n_{i}n_{l}\partial_{k}(n_{l})P_{kj}v_{N}}_{=0}

\displaystyle=n_{i}\partial_{k}(v_{N})P_{kj}={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}v_{N}.

Thus, from (
A.6
), (
A.7
), (
A.8
), equation (
A.4
) follows. Finally, for (
A.5
), applying analogous calculations as in (
A.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}_{n}

\displaystyle=P_{jk}\partial_{k}\big(v_{N}n_{l}\big)P_{li}=\underbrace{P_{jk}\partial_{k}(v_{N})n_{l}P_{li}}_{=0}+P_{jk}\partial_{k}(n_{l})P_{li}v_{N}

\displaystyle=P_{jk}H_{lk}P_{li}v_{N}=H_{ij}v_{N}=\bm{H}v_{N}.

∎

Appendix B
Transport formulae

The following identity, which can be found in
[
30
, Lemma 2.6]
, plays an important role for the transport formula:

(B.1)

\partial^{\circ}\nabla_{\Gamma(t)}g=\nabla_{\Gamma(t)}\partial^{\circ}g-\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\nabla_{\Gamma(t)}g,

where (by our notation in
Section

2.2
and (
A.3
))
\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})
the surface deformation tensor, written as

(B.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})_{ij}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\Gamma})_{i}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})_{j}+n_{i}n_{k}(\nabla_{\Gamma})_{j}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})_{k}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}-{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{t}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}+{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}V_{\Gamma}-(\bm{H}{\mathbf{v}}_{n})^{t})=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}+{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}V_{\Gamma},

where we omit the time argument (t), i.e.
\Gamma(t)=\Gamma
, and where
{\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=V_{\Gamma}
by (
eNS
).

Lemma B.1

(Transport formulae I)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(t)
be an evolving surface with normal velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}=V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
. Assume that the functions
f,\,g
and the vector field
{\mathbf{w}}
are sufficiently smooth, so that all the following quantities exist, then

(B.3)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}g\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\partial^{\circ}g+g\,\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{v}}_{n})\,d\sigma,

(B.4)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}g\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}g+{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial^{\circ}g+{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma}^{\text{div}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\nabla_{\Gamma}g\,d\sigma,

\displaystyle\hskip-149.37697pt\text{ where the tensor }\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma}^{\text{div}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})=\mathrm{I}\,\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})-\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\,(\mathrm{I}\text{ the }3\times 3\text{ Identity matrix})\text{ and,}

(B.5)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}g\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial^{\circ}f\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}g+\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial^{\circ}g\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\ +\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma})\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}g\,d\sigma,

where the tensor
\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma})
is given by

(B.6)

\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma})=\partial^{\circ}\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}-2D({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}),

with the deformation tensor defined by

D({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma})_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}^{t})
.

Proof.

The proof (
B.3
), (
B.4
), (
B.5
) are given in
[
34
, Lemma A.1]
, or
[
28
, Lemma 2.1, Lemma 4.2]
.
∎

Lemma B.2

(Transport formulae II)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(t)
be an evolving surface with normal velocity
{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}=V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
. Assume that the the vector fields
{\mathbf{w}},\,{\mathbf{v}}
are sufficiently smooth, so that all the following quantities exist, then the following transport formulae hold

(B.7)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma+d({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

(B.8)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma+d_{1}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

(B.9)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma+d_{2}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

(B.10)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma+d_{3}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

where
d({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),\,d_{1}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),\,d_{2}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),\,d_{3}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})
are given by

(B.11)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!d(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\,d\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\,d\sigma,

(B.12)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!d_{1}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,

(B.13)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!d_{2}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\big(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\big)\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{v}}\big(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\big)\,d\sigma,

(B.14)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!d_{3}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{w}}\big(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\big):\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{w}}:\big(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\big)\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,

where the tensor
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})
is given by

(B.15)

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}):=\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}-E({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}),

with
E(\cdot)
the rate-of-strain tensor and
D({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})=E({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})
. Notice, also, that
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})
is symmetric.

Proof.

Let us prove (
B.7
) now, then the rest of the transport formulae is proven in an similar manner, using transpose properties. Considering the formula (
B.1
) for vector-valued functions we see that

\partial^{\circ}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}-\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}^{t}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}),

and therefore, with similar calculations to (
B.5
) (see
[
28
, Lemma 2.1]
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}:{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\partial^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\big(\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}-\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}\big):\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\partial^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\big(\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}}-\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}\big)\,d\sigma+\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}\,d\sigma,

from which our desired estimate can be derived, since
\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma
and

\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}:\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{t}{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N}\,d\sigma,

where we also make use of the fact that
D({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N},{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})=E({\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N})
, the rate-of-strain tensor,
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\cdot)=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}(\cdot)
and
\partial^{\circ}({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})
is symmetric. This gives (
B.7
). The rest (
B.8
) – (
B.10
) can be derived similarly.
∎

In our case, we want to find a transport theorem w.r.t. the rate-of-strain tensor
E(\cdot)
. This is much more complicated since
E({\mathbf{w}})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{w}})
, but notice that we have calculated the transport property of all the possible combinations in (
B.7
) – (
B.10
). Based on these results, we can easily derive the following explicit expression. We also, require an appropriate uniform in time bounds.

Lemma B.3

(Transport formulae III)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(t)
be an evolving surface with normal velocity
{\mathbf{v}}_{n}=V_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
. Assume that the vector fields
{\mathbf{w}},\,{\mathbf{v}}
are sufficiently smooth, so that all the following quantities exist, then

(B.16)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}E({\mathbf{w}}):E({\mathbf{v}})\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}(t)}E(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{w}}):E({\mathbf{v}})+E({\mathbf{w}}):E(\partial^{\circ}{\mathbf{v}})\,d\sigma

\displaystyle+{\mathbf{d}}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}),

where by
{\mathbf{d}}(t;\cdot;\cdot,\cdot)
we denote the expression

(B.17)

{\mathbf{d}}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})=\frac{1}{4}\big(d(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})+d_{1}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})+d_{2}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})+d_{3}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})\big),

which is bounded uniform in time as:

(B.18)

{\mathbf{d}}(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})\leq c||{\mathbf{w}}||_{a_{S}}||{\mathbf{v}}||_{a_{S}}.

Proof.

The equality (
B.16
) can be easily proved by accounting for (
B.7
) – (
B.10
). As for the uniform bound of
{\mathbf{d}}(t;\cdot;\cdot,\cdot)
, one can look at (
B.11
), where the smoothness of
\mathcal{M}(t)
, the symmetry of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}
and properties of the spectral and frobenious norms give us

\displaystyle d(t;{\mathbf{V}}_{\Gamma}^{N};{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})\leq c||\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{w}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}||\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}.

Then a straightforward extension gives the desired result for the rest
d_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
(
B.12
) – (
B.14
).
∎
